Deployment Release

16 Deployment Release

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a machine that completes every trial still lack the evidence to be allowed among people?

A successful trial shows what a machine did under the conditions it happened to meet, while permission to operate is a claim about every condition it may meet next. Thousands of clean runs on a dry floor say nothing about the stop on a floor no trial touched, and a brake that stopped the machine last month may not stop it inside the same distance after its lining wears. A machine granted authority on demonstrations therefore carries untested premises into service, where the first test of each one is the event it was meant to prevent. Deciding to deploy requires stating which physical claims the evidence supports, under which conditions and for how long, refusing or narrowing operation wherever a premise remains unknown, and withdrawing the grant when brakes wear, sensors shift, or software changes. At the causal boundary, authority must expire when its physical justification does, because a later software correction cannot undo motion already produced.

Learning Objectives
  • Construct a claim-argument-evidence (CAE) safety case as an auditable engineering assertion DAG linking bounded operating envelopes to qualified measurements, analysis, and operating conditions
  • Distinguish input provenance from premise status, and select the verdict (UNCOND, COND, or REFUSE) the supported operating claim permits
  • Join task-success, cycle-time, and intervention evidence to the physical measurements and restrictions supporting a conditional release
  • Specify standing-condition cadences and validated responses, from brake wear to calibration drift, separately from execution leases
  • Diagnose unsupported warrants, hidden contextual assumptions, and compound defeaters through adversarial review of a release argument
  • Specify the records a release manifest binds by digest and the conditions under which its gate refuses motion
  • Determine which claims a policy or software update reopens, and why shadow operation cannot substitute for closed-loop qualification
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

16.1 The Decision to Deploy

Verification left the machine with a signed set of fault records. Each pairs an observed trace under an injected fault (a stalled proposer, a stale frame, lost fieldbus frames, a sagging bus) with an oracle fixed before the trial, and the set names the operating regimes no trial reached. Beside it sit the records the earlier chapters produced: the authority record of arbitration and fallback outcomes, the placement record of compute scheduling and isolation, and the evaluation record of closed-loop task performance. Those records say how the machine behaved under the conditions tested. They do not say which operating claim the machine may now be granted, because a fault record proves nothing outside the envelope it sampled, and an untested mode is a gap rather than a pass. Offline validation accuracy and clean laboratory demonstrations sit in the same position. Release is the step that decides, claim by claim, what this evidence licenses across a named operational design domain.

↩︎ The empirical verification evidence backing release claims is established in Fault Injection on Hardware.

That step is a threshold every machine operating among humans eventually crosses, the point where builders formally grant it authority to act outside the laboratory. In conventional software engineering, deployment is largely reversible; an operator can recall a defective build within minutes, bounding the cost of failure to the time required to roll back the binary. For physical systems, that release grant differs. Every action authorized by the runtime delivers energy across the causal boundary into the physical environment. When a release decision errs, the failure appears as an actuator driving past an obstacle clearance line or a gripper exerting uncalibrated force against human tissue, and no software rollback can recall kinetic energy already transferred. Aerospace, rail transit, and industrial robotics manage this risk through an explicit safety case, a structured and auditable argument stating what the machine is claimed to do, under what operating conditions, and on what evidence. Physical AI inherits this burden with an added difficulty. A learned policy cannot be proven safe by inspecting its weights, and finite field trials cannot surface every rare tail failure, which is where the worst consequences lie.

The warehouse mobile manipulator shows why past operational success cannot by itself license deployment. In an active logistics depot it must travel narrow aisles past protruding rack ends, unlatch a spring-latched cage door, and hand a ceramic mug to a human coworker. Each subtask turns on a distinct physical claim: a stopping distance inside the clear distance at maximum payload, a bounded peak force on the latch, and a limited contact force during the handover. A spotless test run shows only that the machine executed those motions under the exact conditions of that single trial. A sound release decision must instead bound the physical variations the machine will face in production and show that verified plant dynamics and low-level safety interlocks keep risk within limits across that entire envelope.

A deployed system must also do useful work inside its certified envelope. Closed-loop evaluation logs show whether an end-to-end policy completes its tasks, how often humans must intervene, and which environmental conditions cause the policy to break down. Engineers must weigh these task metrics against the speed limits, payload caps, and spatial restrictions required by the safety case. A mobile base that trips safety stops every few meters avoids collisions while failing its service target; conversely, an agile policy that finishes every nominal trial cannot be given actuator authority until deterministic guardrails enforce safe bounds during perception failures. The release decision brings these two requirements together into one operating profile.

↩︎ The closed-loop evaluation record of task success, intervention rates, and breakdown conditions is established in The Evaluation Record.

Because different facilities have different lighting, floor friction, and foot traffic, an initial release case applies to a single facility, drawing on its site clearance surveys, loaded braking trials, calibrated impact-force measurements, and cryptographically signed fault records. Rather than boiling deployment readiness down to a single benchmark score, the release decision is a bounded, revocable judgment that determines which operational claims the available evidence actually supports, under what physical conditions, and for how long. This chapter builds that decision in the order an adjudicator meets it: the claims are first written as testable physical inequalities over a bounded operating envelope, then organized into an argument whose every link names its evidence. The argument resolves into one of three verdicts (full operational authorization, restricted operation inside enforced limits, or refusal), and standing conditions define when a granted verdict expires. A worked adjudication applies all of this to the running machine, an adversarial review tries to break whatever case survives, and a signed release manifest binds the final decision to the executing machine so that every later software update, retrained checkpoint, or hardware modification passes through the same gate.

↩︎ The signed fault records a release case consumes are established in Fault Records for Release.

16.2 Physical Criteria for Release

A deployment review that signs off on aggregate benchmark scores or smooth demo videos leaves the machine with no testable rule for when it may move. At a rack end, the base must stop inside the available clearance; during handover, the arm must stay within the assessed contact-force limit; and both claims depend on the permission path finishing its check before its deadline. Together, those requirements give release authorization three testable physical criteria: \[\StoppingDistance(\SpeedAtDetection, \PrebrakeDelay, \CredibleBrakingDeceleration, \LocalizationBound, \TrackingErrorBound) \le \ClearDistance, \quad \CollaborativeContactForce(\SpeedAtDetection, \EffectiveImpactMass, \HertzianContactStiffness) \le \AssessedContactForceLimit, \quad \WorstCasePermissionCycleTime \le \CompletionDeadline. \tag{1}\]

The first term abbreviates the full stopping budget of equation 12.1, whose protective margin is charged as well. In the third criterion, \(\WorstCasePermissionCycleTime\) is the worst-case execution time of one permission-path cycle and \(\CompletionDeadline\) the deadline that cycle must meet inside its tick (Execution timing in the sub-millisecond loop). A permission computed after its deadline cannot be credited for that cycle, and the qualified fallback must contain the resulting delay.

Because each criterion is a physical inequality, release authorization cannot rest on intuition, demonstrations, or aggregate benchmark scores. Authorizing deployment requires an auditable safety argument that links every inequality to measured observation ages, worst-case execution time bounds, empirical braking and contact trials, and a validated fallback. These physical bounds also depend on the individual machine’s calibration: its camera and lidar sensor transforms, joint encoder zero offsets, and motor torque constants. A lost or drifted calibration invalidates the certified stopping budget even when software binaries remain unchanged. A sensor mount that shifts in service erodes the localization allowance \(\LocalizationBound\) (section 16.5.1), and a firmware update that overwrites measured sensor extrinsics and joint offsets invalidates the budget just as quickly. The deployment pipeline must therefore preserve and re-verify calibration records before restoring the operating permit (section 16.9.1).

For the warehouse mobile manipulator, the release question asks whether empirical and analytical evidence satisfies these criteria for each of its three tasks inside a scoped operating envelope. The release case defines the assessed physical bounds, the specified hardware fault model, and the validated permission and fallback ladder. This argument is the safety case, which section 16.3.1 gives a formal structure, and it justifies granting a revocable operating permit while those physical conditions hold. The permit is the signed authority under which the permission path may admit the policy’s tracking commands (section 16.4). Finite test runs cannot establish protection against uncharacterized or unmodeled hazards.

A release case is scoped by the operating envelope: the target operational design domain (ODD) paired with the machine’s internal hardware, thermal, and timing invariants. Deployment release authorizes a system configuration under standing operating conditions, complementing rather than replacing the short execution leases that bound individual action chunks. Site inspections, calibration checks, and per-tick permission checks operate at the distinct cadences dictated by their respective failure mechanisms. The envelope also fixes what each of the three possible verdicts, defined in section 16.4, refers to. Unconditional operation (UNCOND) covers the full verified envelope. Conditional operation (COND) covers a separately evidenced subset with constrained kinematics or environmental fences. Refusal (REFUSE) covers none of it. For the mobile manipulator, the proposed nominal operating envelope specifies aisle travel up to 1.3 m/s (chosen; see the Reader Guide) with the tote rack loaded to at most 50 kg. It also specifies floors surveyed to a friction coefficient of at least 0.204 and travel past rack ends with at least 1.10 m of clear distance. The friction floor is an optimistic screen obtained by dividing the required braking deceleration by standard gravity under the assumption that all vehicle weight rests on the driven wheels. Because passive casters carry a share of the normal load without contributing braking force, the driven wheels must generate the total stopping force from a smaller normal load, so actual qualification must include loaded wheel forces, drive settings, and stopping traces. For manipulation, the proposed envelope specifies door approaches at 0.03 m/s and coworker handovers at 0.10 m/s tool center point (TCP) speed.

↩︎ The operational design domain and its known absences are established in Choosing a tractable coverage table.

Inside these boundaries, the learned neural policy proposes actions across the nominal task envelope, but every proposed action requires independent, per-tick permission from an isolated safety enforcer. Mode-specific conditions (table 1) narrow that envelope. The takeover analysis of Clearance-derived fallback deadlines supplies candidate intervention speeds, screening an in-loop request at 0.75 m/s and an out-of-loop crawl at 0.15 m/s. Neither screen qualifies as a standalone release limit, because each depends on a human response time that the release case has not measured. The in-loop slow-down holds only for an operator answering an expected stimulus, and the out-of-loop crawl already fails the 99th-percentile response. The robust design therefore runs the base’s own local stop first and hands over control from standstill, where waiting costs time but no clearance. The restricted mode of a COND verdict narrows the envelope further. On a floor characterized only through the site inspection regime, the machine runs at 1 m/s, below the 1.23 m/s optimistic all-weight inspected-floor screen, while still charging dispatch-to-admission delay and maintaining a positive stopping reserve.

The rules in table 1 follow a strict priority ladder: refusal conditions override mode-specific restrictions, which in turn override nominal limits. Because speed ranges overlap across operational contexts, these thresholds define operating modes rather than disjoint numerical intervals. The payload row shows how a mode differs from an interval. If the tote-rack load estimate has gone stale since the last pick, the base may not transit, because its stopping budget assumes a known mass. The stationary arm may keep working under its own separately evidenced claim. An invalid estimate or a measured overload refuses both. A conditional state restricts admitted proposals through enforcer clamping. A refusal state revokes the operating permit, so the permission path admits no further tracking commands, and commands a validated fallback trajectory. Onboard wheel slip estimation cannot provide prospective clearance guarantees because it detects low friction only after traction has already been lost. A thin film of clear oil (\(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \approx\) 0.05) can be visually indistinguishable from dry concrete to the navigation cameras. By the time a loaded base moving at aisle speed (1.3 m/s) initiates braking on an oil patch and measures slip, it has already committed to a stopping distance that exceeds the clear distance. Consequently, the release case must justify how site surveys, contamination exclusion, and bounded inspection and response procedures preserve the assessed friction floor throughout the authorized interval. Periodic surveys alone cannot exclude a spill between inspections.

↩︎ The ladder of resident fallback responses that a refusal commands is established in The Fallback Ladder.

The safety case must also document machine behavior when operation encounters an envelope boundary. Event-triggered edge telemetry freezes circular buffer traces for post-incident review (section 16.9.5), while the deterministic permission path enforces physical boundaries in real time. Table 1 summarizes the physical dimensions maintained by runtime monitors and administrative site controls. Internal environmental conditions, such as ambient temperature, DC bus rail voltage, and compute system-on-chip (SoC) utilization, enter the release case through fault injection and stress tests conducted across their qualified ranges.

↩︎ The internal qualification ranges for temperature, bus voltage, and compute load that the release case inherits are established in The Operating Envelope.

The nominal latch row in table 1 scopes the baseline policy release case. Although the separately stipulated recovery campaign in Qualifying a recovery update evaluates a changed candidate across nominal and displaced-plate conditions, that experimental provenance does not silently extend the contact-force or timing bounds established here. Deploying the recovery policy requires its own evaluation and safety case update.

A defensible release claim must specify its plant dynamics, assumed contact geometry, and modeled fault set. The release case for the mobile manipulator rests on three criteria. The base stops inside the 1.10 m clear distance before reaching a pedestrian who has stepped out at a rack end and stopped. The arm unlatches the door without exceeding the 100 N structural limit. An item handover does not exceed 50 N of peak contact force against the coworker. The base’s stopping claim assumes that the person, once in the path, stands still. A person who keeps walking toward the moving base closes the gap during the whole reaction and braking interval. As Sizing the composite stopping budget showed, sizing the budget for that approach would cut the aisle speed to the much lower walking-person ceiling. The site therefore controls aisle-end crossings procedurally, and that site crossing rule is itself a premise the release case must evidence. A complete release argument demonstrates which human contacts are excluded before impact, which detected contacts fall within the compliance model, and which single-point faults trigger the isolated fallback. Halting motion after impact occurs does not reduce the initial shock force.

The contact criteria arrive as premises from the enforcement record rather than as calculations derived during release review. The spring-mass impact peak that grows linearly with approach speed (equation 6.7) was first met as the soft-spring exploit of the latch simulator. Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers specializes it to an unbraked elastic contact with a coworker’s hand (equation 12.13), using the arm’s effective mass and the ISO/TS 15066 biomechanical hand model.1 The release case evaluates the operational consequences of those physical results. At the arm’s maximum free-space limit of 1 m/s, even the transient impact model predicts 207 N, exceeding the 50 N criterion by a factor of 4.1×. Consequently, unseparated coworker proximity cannot be permitted at full free-space speed without additional physical containment. For a free hand that recoils upon impact, the criterion admits approach speeds up to 0.24 m/s. At the chosen handover speed of 0.10 m/s, the model predicts a transient force of 20.7 N, below the case criterion. If the coworker’s hand is clamped against a rack upright or structural frame, however, the ceiling drops to 0.053 m/s. At that same 0.10 m/s approach speed, a clamped collision generates 94.9 N, which breaches the limit. The handover claim is therefore strictly a transient-contact claim, inheriting two premises: that the lumped-mass body model and effective mass accurately describe the collision, and that the handover geometry excludes pinching or clamping hazards. Because physical calculations alone cannot guarantee workspace clearance, the release case must establish both premises through empirical evidence and site inspection.

1 ISO/TS 15066 Values as Case Criteria: The document supplies a body model and body-region force and pressure data for transient and quasi-static contact (International Organization for Standardization 2016). ISO 10218-2:2025 incorporates the collaborative-application requirements formerly published as ISO/TS 15066; ISO/TS 15066:2016 itself remains published and is under revision (International Organization for Standardization 2025b). The 50 N handover criterion is none of those tabulated values and not an injury boundary; the site assessment chooses it for one body region, contact type, and geometry, so a change to any of the three reopens the claim instead of inheriting its margin.

International Organization for Standardization. 2016. ISO/TS 15066:2016: Robots and Robotic Devices, Collaborative Robots. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2025b. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.

Unlike the arm’s instantaneous force criteria, the mobile base’s claim is an integrated distance budget. Validating this claim requires empirical evidence bounding the perception observation age, communication bus latencies, and execution lease durations. Sizing the composite stopping budget sized that budget term by term at aisle speed (The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget), integrating equation 12.1 with the jerk-limited \(\Glyph{C}^2\) braking profile from The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking, and found the spare and the reserve that dispatch-to-admission travel leaves. Release inherits those terms as premises. In deployment authorization, the critical engineering metric is the margin of clear distance that remains unspent, and the question release adds is how large an error in a single input would spend it. Notebook 16.1 recapitulates the enforcement budget and adds that question as step 4.

Napkin Math 16.1: The aisle spare
Problem: At the 1.3 m/s aisle speed, how much of the 1.10 m clear distance does the stopping budget leave unspent, what error in a single input would spend it, and how much remains once dispatch-to-admission work is charged?

Variables:

  • Aisle speed \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.3 m/s (Chosen); clear distance \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (Illustrative, standing in for a site survey)
  • Pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay =\) 133.6 ms (Derived from illustrative terms: 51.6 ms observation age plus 82 ms from the last valid renewal to brake onset)
  • Credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (Illustrative) and jerk bound \(\JerkLimit =\) 7.11 m/s³ (Derived, The C^2 stopping suffix and jerk-limited braking)
  • Allowances \(\LocalizationBound =\) 50 mm and \(\TrackingErrorBound =\) 40 mm (Illustrative); margin \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin =\) 100 mm (Chosen)
  • Dispatch-to-admission allocation \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 63.3 ms (Illustrative allocation, The board-link budget and the dispatch-to-admission interval)

Math:

  1. Reaction: \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay =\) 1.3 m/s \(\times\) 133.6 ms \(=\) 173.7 mm.
  2. Braking: Because \(\SpeedAtDetection \ge \CredibleBrakingDeceleration^2/\JerkLimit\), the stop reaches full deceleration, and \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration) + \SpeedAtDetection\,\CredibleBrakingDeceleration/(2\JerkLimit) =\) 422.5 mm \(+\) 182.8 mm \(=\) 605.3 mm.
  3. Spare: 173.7 mm \(+\) 605.3 mm \(+\) 50 mm \(+\) 40 mm \(+\) 100 mm \(=\) 969.0 mm, leaving 1,100 mm \(-\) 969.0 mm \(=\) 131.0 mm.
  4. One input wrong: As extra delay, 131.0 mm \(/\) 1.3 m/s \(=\) 100.8 ms. As weaker braking, setting step 2 equal to the 736.3 mm braking room gives \(\BreakEvenBrakingDeceleration =\) 1.39 m/s², a 31 percent shortfall.
  5. Admission charged: \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\DispatchAdmissionInterval =\) 1.3 m/s \(\times\) 63.3 ms \(=\) 82.3 mm, leaving a reserve of 48.7 mm, or 37.5 ms at aisle speed.

Result: The budget spends all but 131.0 mm of the clear distance before admission work, and all but 48.7 mm after it.

Systems insight: The validity of the stopping guarantee \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\) hinges on the narrow margin of unspent distance rather than the nominal distance consumed. Measured against the partial spare of step 3, a credible deceleration overstated by 31 percent spends the whole of it. The reserve that step 5 leaves after admission work is less than half that spare, so once \(\DispatchAdmissionInterval\) is charged a smaller braking error suffices.

This reserve must also absorb any unmodeled runtime uncertainty, so it cannot be treated as extra allowable latency or repurposed as a margin for control handovers. The reserve left after admission work, 37.5 ms at aisle speed, tolerates less unbudgeted delay or braking shortfall than the break-even values of step 4. Unless each component of the timing and kinematic chain is measured, nothing shows that the reserve survives the latencies and friction variation of a production floor. Each of the three criteria (stop, latch, and handover) therefore depends directly on premises about achievable deceleration, sensor-to-actuator latency, and contact stiffness that no calculation can certify. Because these physical properties can degrade over mechanical operating life, an auditable safety case must expose these runtime dependencies so that certification engineers can judge whether the empirical evidence supports deployment.

The partial budget leaves only 131.0 mm of the 1.10 m clear distance spare.

16.3 Safety Cases and Claims

A photograph of a clear aisle cannot establish that a loaded mobile base will stop before reaching an obstacle. A valid stopping claim depends on guaranteed physical clearance, actual braking capability, and end-to-end response timing, with each parameter supported by empirical evidence gathered under documented operating conditions. A safety case connects these physical premises to the release claim step by step, so an assessor can see which guarantees rest on measurements and which remain unverified assumptions.

16.3.1 Claims, arguments, and warrants

A bundle of test logs and benchmark plots gives a release reviewer no way to tell which physical claims the data actually support or where an unstated assumption hides. A safety case is auditable only when its logic is written down in a form that another engineer can inspect and challenge link by link. Two notations are widely used in practice, and both capture the same structure. The claim-argument-evidence (CAE) framework divides the case into three parts. The claim is a statement about system behavior that is either true or false. The argument is the causal or inductive reasoning connecting the claim to retained support. The evidence consists of the calibrated measurements, justified analyses, and validated operating procedures (Bishop and Bloomfield 1998; Bloomfield and Netkachova 2014). Goal Structuring Notation (GSN) draws the same structure as a directed graph: top-level goals (claims) split through explicit strategies (arguments) into concrete solutions (evidence items). Context and assumption nodes state the operating envelope within which each link holds (Kelly 1998; Kelly and Weaver 2004). The fault injection records enter this graph directly as solutions supporting each named sub-claim. Because release adjudication turns an assurance graph into a binding operational authorization, the safety case of definition 16.1 evaluates directly to an enforceable release verdict.

Bloomfield, Robin, and Kateryna Netkachova. 2014. “Building Blocks for Assurance Cases.” IEEE International Symposium on Software Reliability Engineering Workshops (ISSREW), 186–91. https://doi.org/10.1109/ISSREW.2014.72.
Kelly, Tim, and Rob Weaver. 2004. The Goal Structuring Notation: A Safety Argument Notation.

↩︎ The fault-record set, in which every record names the one release claim it supports, is established in The fault-record set specification.

2 Toulmin’s Argument Anatomy: The Uses of Argument (Toulmin 1958) splits practical reasoning into claim, data, warrant, backing, qualifier, and rebuttal. Safety cases preserve this anatomy: evidence supplies the data, stated confidence acts as the qualifier, and the rebuttal becomes the defeater evaluated during adversarial review (section 16.7).

Toulmin, Stephen E. 1958. The Uses of Argument. Cambridge University Press.
Koopman, Philip. 2023. “UL 4600: What to Include in an Autonomous Vehicle Safety Case.” Computer 56 (5): 101–4. https://doi.org/10.1109/MC.2023.3236171.
Favaro, Francesca, Laura Fraade-Blanar, Scott Schnelle, Trent Victor, Mauricio Peña, Johan Engstrom, John Scanlon, Kris Kusano, and Dan Smith. 2023. “Building a Credible Case for Safety: Waymo’s Approach for the Determination of Absence of Unreasonable Risk.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2306.01917.

This decomposition comes from Stephen Toulmin’s model of practical argumentation2 (Toulmin 1958). In industrial practice, standards such as ANSI/UL 4600 require goal-based safety cases for autonomous physical products (UL Standards & Engagement 2023; Koopman 2023). Production autonomous fleets use this same structure. For example, Waymo organizes the safety case for its driverless ride-hailing operations around layered, dynamic claims showing the absence of unreasonable risk, arguing separately that its risk acceptance criteria are complete and that their empirical evaluation is sound (Favaro et al. 2023). For an embodied robot, the root claim \(\AssuranceClaim_0\) states a testable physical invariant, such as a stopping distance inside the surveyed aisle clearance (\(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\)) or a bounded collaborative contact force (\(\CollaborativeContactForce \le \AssessedContactForceLimit\)). Supporting sub-claims name the required mechanical, electrical, and computational conditions, and retained measurements, derivations, or validated procedures back each leaf. Writing these relationships as a directed acyclic graph (DAG) exposes intermediate dependencies and rules out circular reasoning, so a reviewer can trace any operational claim down to its physical measurements and operating assumptions.

Definition 16.1: Claim-argument-evidence safety case

Claim-argument-evidence safety case is a structured argument that a named machine, in a stated operating envelope and under a stated fault model, meets explicit physical criteria, written as claims that decompose through stated arguments into retained evidence.

  1. Significance: It makes a release decision checkable one link at a time. For the warehouse machine, the three inequalities of equation 1 become six claims whose leaves are individual premises. A reviewer can find the one premise, such as a braking deceleration no loaded trial has measured, on which the verdict turns.
  2. Distinction: A test report or benchmark score records what happened under the conditions sampled. A safety case states why that evidence supports a claim about conditions the machine has not yet met, and names the assumptions under which the reasoning stops holding.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that the case is paperwork assembled after development for a regulator. It is written with the design, and in this book’s architecture its verdict decides what the permission path lets the machine do.

The soundness of this argument DAG depends on keeping evidence distinct from demonstration. A demonstration records successful task execution across an uncharacterized test set, such as a string of clean door openings with the strike plate at its nominal position or a thousand collision-free warehouse runs. Demonstrations show nominal performance across observed runs, but they give no mathematical or statistical bounds on tail behavior, sensor faults, or corner cases outside those logged runs. Evidence, by contrast, consists of falsifiable, calibrated records with stated uncertainty bounds: an execution-time distribution measured on a hardware logic analyzer paired with worst-case execution time (WCET) bounds, loaded stopping trials across degraded friction surfaces, or hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) fault-injection traces at envelope corners. The evidence must match the scope of the claim. Nominal trials can support nominal capability claims, and calibration logs can validate sensor coordinate frames, but bounding an envelope limit or worst-case response requires systematic coverage, stated uncertainty margins, and an explicit warrant.

Connecting evidence to a claim requires a warrant, the reasoning principle that explains why the measured data support the claim. For a learned neural policy, teams often reach for an inductive warrant, arguing from thousands of failure-free test episodes to future operational reliability. The exposure wall analyzed in Verification Ceilings and the Exposure Wall shows why purely inductive warrants cannot support catastrophic hazard claims. At 95 percent confidence the zero-failure exposure wall of equation 7.9 becomes \(\Time \ge 3.0/\ConstantHazardRate\) (because \(\ln 20 \approx 3.0\)), where \(\ConstantHazardRate\) is the failure rate per operating hour to be demonstrated. Statistically proving an ultra-low failure rate of \(10^{-9}\) per operating hour therefore requires billions of operating hours without a single failure (Butler and Finelli 1993; Kalra and Paddock 2016). Deciding what target rate is acceptable in the first place is an engineering and societal judgment that empirical testing cannot make on its own (Koopman 2022).

Koopman, Philip. 2022. How Safe Is Safe Enough? Measuring and Predicting Autonomous Vehicle Safety. Independently published.
Hawkins, Richard, Colin Paterson, Chiara Picardi, Yan Jia, Radu Calinescu, and Ibrahim Habli. 2021. “Guidance on the Assurance of Machine Learning in Autonomous Systems (AMLAS).” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2102.01564.

A sound safety case bridges this gap with deterministic architectural warrants. Rather than betting physical safety on policy generalization, the system grounds its top-level safety claims in an independent permission path. That path evaluates the control barrier functions of Safe Sets as Conditional Permission to clamp actuator commands, and commands an assessed safe stop whenever a learned proposal approaches the boundary of the viable set \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\). The architectural warrant narrows the claim rather than escaping physical limits. The permission path protects only against monitored hazards that the actuators can arrest within the available stopping clearance. The learned proposer still needs its own operational argument to show task capability and handle unmonitored tasks. Recent standards formalize these complementary roles. ISO/PAS 8800:2024 adapts automotive safety lifecycles, data curation rules, and assurance arguments to AI components (International Organization for Standardization 2024b). The Assurance of Machine Learning in Autonomous Systems (AMLAS) framework supplies safety-case patterns for machine learning pipelines across autonomous systems (Hawkins et al. 2021). In this book’s architecture, the permission path carries the safety argument for all monitored physical hazards. It anchors its formal claims to a rated watchdog and to the certified functions beneath it in the drives, the safety-rated laser scanner, and the arm’s safety controller (section 16.3.4). The learned proposer’s argument can then focus on operational availability and unmonitored mission tasks.

↪ How much an architectural shield’s per-command guarantee dilutes into a system-level hazard rate is developed in Conditional shield credit and common-cause bounds.

16.3.2 Contextual assumptions and the weakest link

A stopping test passed on clean, dry concrete under bright warehouse lamps can fail the first time the robot enters a dim aisle if the safety case never stated the lighting assumption behind its camera exposure time. Every physical measurement rests on ambient conditions, and moving the machine into a dimly lit aisle can lengthen camera exposure and observation age even when the mechanical brakes and tires behave identically. An assurance argument must state these contextual assumptions explicitly so that measurements gathered under one set of conditions are never credited outside their verified operating envelope.

When an operating assumption is left unstated, safety depends entirely on whether a downstream check happens to catch the resulting deviation. In the mobile manipulator’s stopping budget, the warrant quietly assumes that warehouse illumination permits the navigation camera to operate at its nominal exposure. If dim lighting doubles that exposure time from 16 ms to 32 ms, the observation arrives 8 ms older than assumed (measuring exposure age from mid-frame). Left unchecked, this additional latency translates to 10.4 mm of extra travel at the robot’s 1.3 m/s aisle velocity, consuming roughly 8 percent of the budget’s 131.0 mm margin. On this machine the permission path’s frame admission age check, the freshness test of the observation contract (The Observation Contract), rejects the delayed frames. Because an active chunk can execute only while its evidence and lease remain valid, the robot instead halts in an aisle where the design assumed it could run. The team meets the unstated lighting assumption as unexplained availability loss rather than as a collision, and what kept the blind spot from eroding clearance was a latency check that knows nothing about illumination. A design without that check would have spent the margin silently.

Assumptions that lack runtime monitors carry far greater danger. Mechanical drift in a camera mount can introduce steady-state calibration offsets while every frame passes freshness and timestamp validations. The safety case must explicitly link localization tolerances to calibration routines and physical inspection logs. Treating fresh telemetry as proof of accurate spatial calibration leaves that warrant unsupported.

↪ Premises that no sensor on the machine can observe, and the restrictions they force on a release claim, are developed in What the release claim rests on.

A safety case is not an aggregate scorecard in which high performance in one branch compensates for a deficit in another. Benchmark accuracy on an object recognition dataset cannot offset an uncharacterized latency tail on a CAN FD (Controller Area Network with Flexible Data-Rate) bus, nor can millions of simulated training episodes compensate for missing brake-wear measurements. When every child claim is required, the argument is conjunctive, and it is no stronger than its weakest required premise. In practice it is usually weaker, because doubt across independent premises accumulates. For a stipulated probability model, let each event mean that one required premise is valid under its stated conditions, and let \(\RequiredPremiseConfidence_1, \dots, \RequiredPremiseConfidence_n\) be the assigned event probabilities. These are not finite-trial confidence levels and estimate neither this robot’s measured hazard rate nor calibrated assurance confidence. Define the illustrative claim event \(\AssuranceClaim\) exactly as the conjunction of these independent events; its probability is their product: \[\Pr(\AssuranceClaim) = \prod_{i=1}^{\RequiredPremiseCount} \RequiredPremiseConfidence_i. \tag{2}\]

If each of 10 required premises holds with assigned probability 0.99, the conjunction holds with only about 0.90, lower than any individual premise. The mobile base’s stopping claim is conjunctive in exactly this way. Surveyed clear distance, credible braking deceleration, brake onset latency, observation age, and localization and tracking tolerances all enter a single stopping budget. A flawed warrant in any single term invalidates the root claim, regardless of how thoroughly the other terms are documented. An assessor therefore looks to the least supported required premise and the number of premises sharing the claim, not to the gross volume of supporting documentation.

This conjunctive degradation has one principled engineering exception. An argument can support a claim through two independent legs, either of which suffices on its own, so that the claim fails only if both legs fail simultaneously (Bloomfield and Littlewood 2003). In a separate stipulated model, define \(\AssuranceClaim\) exactly as the union of \(\ArgumentLegCount\) independent sufficient-leg events with assigned probabilities \(\ArgumentLegConfidence_1, \dots, \ArgumentLegConfidence_{\ArgumentLegCount}\). The event holds unless all legs fail: \[\Pr(\AssuranceClaim) = 1 - \prod_{j=1}^{\ArgumentLegCount} (1 - \ArgumentLegConfidence_j). \tag{3}\]

3 Independence of Diverse Legs: When independently written versions of a software specification were evaluated on identical inputs, they failed coincidentally far more frequently than independent failure models predicted, statistically rejecting the independence assumption (Knight and Leveson 1986). Design diversity does not by itself yield independence in failure.

Knight, John C., and Nancy G. Leveson. 1986. “An Experimental Evaluation of the Assumption of Independence in Multiversion Programming.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering SE-12 (1): 96–109. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.1986.6312924.

If each of two independent legs holds with assigned probability 0.90, the combined claim holds with roughly 0.99, well above either leg alone. The equality describes the stipulated event model. If sufficient-leg events merely imply a broader physical claim, their union probability supplies a lower bound rather than automatically equaling the probability of that claim. The third law, proposal is not permission, instead supplies a conditional architectural argument: a policy error alone cannot breach a monitored stopping or contact invariant while the qualified enforcer, plant, sensing, timing, and fault premises hold. Policy validation and per-tick permission checks together do not justify assigning independent probabilities to their success.3 Claim C5 in table 2 requires evidence that shared processors, memory buses, power rails, or sensor pipelines cannot compromise the permission path under the credited fault and interference model. Such isolation evidence addresses common causes within its qualified scope; it does not alone establish every statistical independence assumption. If the event independence is unsupported, the two-leg probability calculation is unavailable; shared causes must be represented explicitly in the argument. The enforcer leg is itself conjunctive across its own physical premises, because claims C1 through C4 must all hold for the enforcer to function. Diverse legs protect against policy failure; they cannot make up for unmeasured brake physics or missing timing margins, so the reviewer must check every leaf against the actual plant.

16.3.3 Matching the argument to the machine

Flawless arithmetic on assumed braking and latency numbers proves only that the robot would stop in time if the numbers were true; it says nothing about whether the physical machine on the floor actually meets them. While analytical stopping models relate brake onset, deceleration, sensor age, and tracking error cleanly on paper, an equation is only as sound as its physical inputs. A release reviewer must check which parameters come from measurements on the production hardware and which remain unverified design assumptions.

For collaborative manipulation tasks, biomechanical thresholds govern the contact force argument once the claim explicitly details the target body region, contact classification (transient vs. quasi-static), pressure distribution, impact geometry, and measurement methodology (International Organization for Standardization 2025b). The mobile base’s claims, which form the core of the release argument, derive directly from empirical stopping distance budgets and site survey logs. Two conceptual frameworks borrowed from automotive engineering organize the fault analysis. Functional safety mitigates hazards arising from hardware malfunctions or systematic software defects (International Organization for Standardization 2018). In contrast, Safety of the Intended Functionality (SOTIF) addresses hazards stemming from performance insufficiencies or specification gaps in the absence of hardware faults, such as an obstacle detector failing to recognize a worker partially obscured by shrink-wrapped pallet staging (International Organization for Standardization 2022). Every leaf of the release argument requires support appropriate to its premise: qualified plant and timing observations, justified analysis, or validated operating restrictions under explicit assumptions. The formal standards governing these subsystems are machinery safety standards, mapped in section 16.3.4 to each component.

Goodenough, John B., Charles B. Weinstock, and Ari Z. Klein. 2013. “Eliminative Induction: A Basis for Arguing System Confidence.” 2013 35th International Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE), 1161–64. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICSE.2013.6606668.

The claim-argument-evidence scorecard in table 2 defines six core release claims, pairing each physical warrant with its adjudication against the evidence developed in this book. Claim C3 shows how a warrant fails. An unnoticed oil slick on a polished concrete floor destroys wheel traction and invalidates the nominal deceleration model. A physically realizable condition that invalidates an assurance warrant in this manner is a defeater (Goodenough et al. 2013). Adversarial safety review hunts for these defeaters (section 16.7). Similarly, Claim C6 depends strictly on test records validating the four supervisory authority handoff rules (A1–A4) established in Per-tick evidence tuples and the fault injection suite. Drawn as an assurance DAG (figure 1), the argument splits C1 into its three physical scenarios (base stop, door latch, mug handover), ends every branch in a premise leaf whose outline records the maturity of its evidence, and attaches the oil-film defeater directly to C3. Because no leaf has been measured on the loaded production configuration, every row of the scorecard evaluates to REFUSE, the verdict that grants no operating permit and commands an assessed stop or hold (section 16.4). Section 16.6 works through why, branch by branch.

Checkpoint 16.1: Safety case decomposition and warrants

Before presenting a safety case for adjudication, test its structure:

The scorecard enforces a strict separation between physical safety requirements and the empirical records available to substantiate them. Even when every internal warrant is traced to a hardware measurement, however, an engineering team does not choose its own risk-reduction hierarchy or protective ratings in a vacuum. The release reviewer must keep this evidentiary discipline across the entire assurance tree while verifying which external machinery and regulatory standards govern each branch.

16.3.4 The standards map for the running machine

Certifying an industrial mobile manipulator against road-vehicle standards, or against fixed-arm standards that ignore a moving base, leaves the hazards of a moving arm on a moving base uncovered. Before authorizing deployment, an assessor must establish which formal standards and regulations govern each subsystem and verify where the corresponding evidence was gathered. Because this running machine is an industrial mobile manipulator rather than an autonomous road vehicle, its formal certification path follows industrial machinery directives rather than automotive standards. ISO 12100 sets the overall risk-management framework. The engineering team identifies hazards, evaluates risk, and applies a three-step reduction hierarchy, with inherently safe design first, engineered safeguards second, and user warnings and operating procedures last (International Organization for Standardization 2010). Every standard in table 3 addresses a specific tier of this hierarchy. Autonomy and automotive standards enter as borrowed engineering methods, supplying vocabulary for learned policies, distribution shifts, and assurance cases that traditional industrial standards are only beginning to adopt.

Two standards in the map require a clear boundary because the running machine straddles mobile robotics and fixed manipulation. ISO 10218-2:2025 governs industrial robot systems and collaborative work cells, but it explicitly excludes the kinetic hazards introduced when a manipulator rides on a mobile platform (International Organization for Standardization 2025b), which is this warehouse machine’s configuration. Conversely, ISO 3691-4 treats the mobile base as a driverless industrial truck. The standard that bridges this gap by treating the mobile manipulator as a single unit is ANSI/A3 R15.08. Mounting an active manipulator on a mobile platform classifies the machine as an Industrial Mobile Robot (IMR) Type C, and Part 2 sets the safety requirements for integrating that combined unit into an active facility (Association for Advancing Automation 2020, 2023). In jurisdictions where R15.08 lacks statutory standing, the combined hazards of mobility and manipulation fall back onto the machine’s primary ISO 12100 risk assessment.

Association for Advancing Automation. 2020. ANSI/A3 R15.08-1-2020: Industrial Mobile Robots, Safety Requirements, Part 1: Requirements for the Industrial Mobile Robot. Association for Advancing Automation.
Association for Advancing Automation. 2023. ANSI/A3 R15.08-2-2023: Industrial Mobile Robots, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Requirements for IMR System(s) and IMR Application(s). Association for Advancing Automation.

A second boundary concerns which layers of the permission path carry formal safety ratings. As Certified safety functions beneath the permission path established, the only rated function within the permission path is the safety MCU’s lease-expiry and heartbeat watchdog, which asserts Safe Torque Off (STO) or Safe Stop 1 (SS1) on the motor drives when valid renewals cease. The admission checks that evaluate neural proposals carry no rating. The certified functions of the motor drives, the safety-rated laser scanner, and the arm’s safety controller sit beneath the permission path. For release, the formal protective claims for every monitored hazard therefore rest on that watchdog and that certified layer. The release argument still uses the software admission checks for operational utility and nominal hazard prevention, but any hazard claim that depends only on unrated software remains an empirical premise rather than a certified safety function.

Statutory compliance reaches physical AI through product-safety law rather than through standalone artificial intelligence rules, and its sharpest consequence for release concerns updates. Under the European Union’s Machinery Regulation (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2023), a substantial modification is any physical or digital change made after deployment that the original manufacturer did not foresee and that introduces a new hazard or raises an existing risk enough to require additional safeguarding. An organization that pushes an unverified over-the-air model update can therefore become, in law, the manufacturer of the modified machine, with every obligation that role carries (section 16.9.1). The same regulation requires third-party conformity assessment4 for machinery whose safety components have fully or partially self-evolving behavior and use machine learning to execute safety functions, so a learned safety function cannot be self-certified. Its essential requirements also constrain learning directly. The limits of each safety function must be documented in the manufacturer’s risk assessment. No rule or parameter the machine generates, even during a learning phase, may change those limits where the change could create a hazardous situation (Annex III, section 1.2.1). Keeping the learned proposer outside the permission path meets that requirement only if the safety argument does not depend on the proposer’s learned behavior. Two neighboring acts reach the same machine. The EU AI Act, Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024a), as amended in July 2026 by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2026), places the Machinery Regulation in Section B of its Annex I, so AI-specific safety rules for machines arrive through the machinery framework. The Commission must adopt delegated acts that insert those rules into the Machinery Regulation’s Annex III and that apply by 2 August 2028. Because the robot receives network updates, it is also a product with digital elements under the Cyber Resilience Act (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024b), which requires vulnerability handling and secure updates throughout the manufacturer’s support period, at least five years unless the product is expected to be in use for less. Its duty to report actively exploited vulnerabilities applies from 11 September 2026, ahead of the rest of the act. Table 3 lists the dates from which the other obligations apply. These milestones do not alter the underlying physics or systems engineering; they set the calendar dates by which statutory authorities will demand an inspectable assurance case.

4 Conformity Assessment: The formal procedure through which a manufacturer certifies that a machine fulfills all essential health and safety requirements before affixing the CE marking (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2023). While manufacturers may self-assess conventional machinery, high-risk systems and self-evolving AI safety components require third-party evaluation by a notified body. Because the conformity assessment path is fixed before market entry, every record in the release manifest must be formatted for independent inspection.

European Parliament and Council of the European Union. 2023. Regulation (EU) 2023/1230 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 14 June 2023 on Machinery and Repealing Directive 2006/42/EC and Council Directive 73/361/EEC. Official Journal of the European Union, L 165, 29.6.2023, p. 1.
European Parliament and Council of the European Union. 2024a. Official Journal of the European Union, L series, 2024/1689, 12.7.2024.

Official Journal of the European Union, L series, 2024/2847, 20.11.2024.

The standards map also marks the boundary between statutory obligations and the systems architecture developed in this book. Formal standards prescribe documented arguments, rated protective functions, and independent third-party assessments. The verdicts, signed capability descriptors, and release manifest developed in the rest of this chapter are one engineering architecture for carrying those obligations into the runtime system. The safety case accepted before deployment then stays binding on the machine long after the inspectors have left the floor.

16.4 The Three Verdicts

Treating a release sign-off as an informal paper memo forces teams either to ground a useful machine over an unmeasured corner case or to wave it onto the floor with unproven assumptions. Every row of table 2 ends in an adjudication, and the release decision resolves the entire assertion DAG into one of three verdicts: unconditional operation, conditional operation, or refusal to operate. In this book’s architecture, these verdicts are cryptographically signed capability descriptors loaded into the real-time safety microcontroller before motor power stages are energized, not advisory certificates or administrative paperwork. A valid descriptor is the machine’s operating permit: while it holds, the permission path may admit the policy’s tracking commands inside the envelope the descriptor names, and when it is revoked or absent, the permission path admits none. Although safety standards do not mandate this specific mechanism (section 16.3.4), the qualified gate and permission path enforce the authenticated descriptor’s limits on physical operation. The first verdict, unconditional operation (UNCOND), authorizes tracking proposals across the full operating envelope specified in a signed release manifest, provided its standing conditions remain continuously verified. Each hazard claim in that safety case requires qualified measurements, justified analysis, or validated operating restrictions applicable to the specified physical plant, payload distribution, operational design domain, and fault set. Brake thermal capacity, stopping clearance, and tracking-error bounds must all be qualified for that envelope, and independent permission checks must stand wherever learned policy proposals can reach physical actuators. The verdict does not eliminate residual or newly emergent hazards; it authorizes operation solely under the assessed claims and requires detection and safe withdrawal within the qualified response budget whenever a physical premise fails.

Definition 16.2: Release verdict

Release verdict is the signed decision that sets how much authority a named machine’s permission path may grant: UNCOND for the full assessed operating envelope, COND for a separately evidenced restricted envelope, or REFUSE when any required premise is missing, contradicted, or unknown, in each case only while its standing conditions hold.

  1. Significance: It turns the weakest premise of the argument into the machine’s operating limit on \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). On this book’s illustrative evidence every claim carries an unknown premise, so the warehouse machine’s verdict is REFUSE. A stipulated set of loaded stopping and floor records would support a COND at 1 m/s on inspected floors, below the 1.23 m/s optimistic all-weight partial ceiling.
  2. Distinction: A test result records the outcome of an individual trial, whereas a degraded mode is an autonomous runtime fallback triggered during execution. By contrast, a release verdict is established before deployment: it names the qualified envelope and the empirical evidence upon which it rests, while leaving the proposal boundary fixed, since the permission path continues to validate every proposed action individually.
  3. Common pitfall: A common misconception is that a conditional verdict (COND) can patch an incomplete safety case by ignoring an unproven claim. A restricted envelope instead demands its own dedicated evidence and analytical limits; an unresolved premise in the primary case leaves that operating mode firmly refused.

16.4.1 Restricted operation and access boundaries

When a safety case lacks evidence for full-speed aisle travel or open-floor contact, letting the robot run under an informal operating note leaves the permission path enforcing the wrong limits. The second verdict, conditional operation (COND), applies when empirical evidence supports the stated hazard claims within a restricted subset of the envelope and binds the permission path to those narrower limits. In the running machine, COND selects a pre-validated safety configuration, much like industrial safety controllers and protective optical scanners switch between pre-validated field sets5 and speed-monitoring profiles. All restricted kinematic thresholds (\(\SpeedBound \le \RestrictedSpeedBound\), \(\ProfileAccelerationBound \le \RestrictedAccelerationBound\)) and spatial geofences are evaluated and validated as one configuration before release; the runtime verdict selects among these vetted profiles rather than inventing parameters on the fly. A conditional verdict (COND) is an offline, predeployment contract that restricts the machine’s operating envelope until re-adjudication. A degraded mode is an online runtime fallback, pre-assessed and named in the signed release, that the permission path invokes when an active subsystem loses telemetry or sensor redundancy (section 16.5.3 sizes one for the base). Release then enforces physical, kinematic, or environmental boundaries that exclude unverified hazard states, such as de-rated joint speeds, a restricted illumination range, or a physical barrier that keeps humans outside the manipulator’s workspace. Where contact force cannot be bounded for unseparated co-presence, a restricted release case may require physical separation: figure 2 illustrates yellow perimeter mesh fencing around an industrial workcell. Crediting that separation requires validated access-interlock and stopping evidence showing that the protective response completes before hazardous access.

5 Safety Scanner Field Sets: A safety laser scanner stores several protective-field geometries that were configured and validated in advance, and a safety controller selects one of them from encoded speed or position inputs. The scanner never computes a new field at runtime. COND borrows the pattern, so the verdict selects a pre-assessed limit set and no untested parameter can reach the permission path.

Fencing excludes unseparated contact by keeping people out of the hazardous volume. The alternative that lets people share the floor, speed and separation monitoring (SSM, Certified safety functions beneath the permission path), needs a different access claim from the stationary-person stopping claim of C1. Its separation clearance must account for human approach velocity across both system reaction time and physical deceleration, robot displacement before brake onset, the executed braking profile, and measurement uncertainty. The warehouse case retains its site crossing rule; engineering teams cannot substitute a stationary-person stopping model into this context and claim to have achieved unseparated, open-floor human-robot collaboration.

Every quantitative condition attached to a release verdict names the operational limit the permission path enforces, maintaining explicit margin below the calculated physical ceiling. Item handover illustrates this margin requirement. The contract signs a TCP speed below the absolute ceiling that the contact criterion admits under equation 12.13. The gap between these two speeds accounts for unobserved physical evolution between discrete sensor samples: velocity growth between sampling instants, encoder quantization noise, and drive lag during an enforcement clamp. A contract signed at the theoretical ceiling leaves zero margin for these physical latency terms, and the release case requires measured empirical bounds on each term before the signed operational speed can be accepted.

The inspected-floor condition follows the same discipline. Where the site inspection regime certifies a friction coefficient of only 0.12, the illustrative all-weight-on-driven-wheels screen uses the optimistic traction ceiling \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity =\) 0.12 \(\times\) 9.81 m/s² \(=\) 1.18 m/s² against the 2 m/s² that the dry-floor budget assumes. Actual loaded credible deceleration also depends on the driven-wheel load share, drive and geometry conditions, and qualified stopping traces. Under the all-weight screening premise, the partial budget that admits 1.44 m/s on surveyed floors admits only 1.23 m/s under this reduced traction. Visual inspection alone cannot establish this lower friction bound because a thin film of transparent oil readily escapes visual detection. The site must qualify both the friction assessment method and the operating procedures (cleaning schedules, spill isolation, and access restrictions) that preserve the assessed floor state between inspections. If that maintenance regime cannot guarantee the exclusion of contaminants over the authorized operating interval, reducing vehicle speed does not salvage the safety case; the aisle remains refused.

The third verdict, refusal to operate (REFUSE), is mandatory whenever the safety case has missing warrants, uncharacterized tail latencies, incomplete fault-injection coverage, or unmonitored failure modes. For systems where torque removal causes immediate physical harm (such as upright bipedal humanoids falling under gravity), REFUSE cannot simply de-energize motor phases to zero torque; it must execute an assessed, powered descent or seated hold. If the safety enforcer’s worst-case execution time under the designated computational placement has never been verified against its 400 μs deadline, or if the fault-injection campaign omitted common-cause failures identified in the deployment topology, the hazard remains unquantified and cannot support release. Refusal is likewise mandatory whenever physical measurements contradict the mathematical model behind a safety budget term, such as loaded stopping trials that yield decelerations below the 2 m/s² assumed by the braking model.

A refusal verdict is an actionable engineering result rather than an administrative dead end. It redirects schedule pressure toward evidence by pointing to the exact broken link in the assertion DAG. If release is refused because loaded stopping trials contradict the braking model, the engineering team does not debate risk tolerance. Developers instead repeat physical braking trials at maximum payload mass until the qualified trial campaign and its uncertainty analysis support a credible lower deceleration bound. They then re-derive the stopping budget from that lower bound and submit for re-adjudication only when the aisle budget closes on validated data. The refusal verdict names the exact empirical test that must pass before re-adjudication can proceed.

Accountability for the release decision must rest with a named lead systems adjudicator rather than being split across development teams. When a vision team signs off on perception metrics, an actuator supplier on torque ratings, and a machine learning team on policy convergence, each group can assume that another layer holds the safety margin. The lead adjudicator alone attests that the integrated, physical machine satisfies the safety case. This sign-off binds the release verdict to a specific set of technical records: cryptographic digests of verified policy weights, calibration logs for manipulator contact-force sensing, empirical brake-onset measurements paired with justified worst-case response bounds, and fully loaded stopping trials establishing credible deceleration limits. The adjudicator signs the release manifest with a private key held inside a hardware security module,6 binding the manifest to the organization’s signing key and sign-off record. The robot runtime checks this digital signature against a public key anchored in its hardware root of trust before enabling actuators.

6 Hardware Security Module (HSM): A tamper-resistant device can generate and retain a non-exportable private key and perform signing inside its own boundary. The signature identifies the signing key; authenticated access, sign-off records, and key-custody controls establish who authorized its use.

↪ The adjudicator’s signature as one of several decisions behind each admitted action, each needing a named owner, is developed in Decisions Behind an Admitted Action.

The adjudicator’s signature records the internal acceptance of the engineering organization, whereas external certification requires independent evaluation. UL 4600 requires independent assessment of the autonomous safety case (UL Standards & Engagement 2023), and machinery whose primary safety functions rely on machine learning falls under regulations, such as the European Union Machinery Regulation, that require third-party conformity assessment (section 16.3.4). An assessor who did not build the safety case brings an independent view to the assumptions that shaped its trade-offs. Organizational separation alone does not establish a probability of coincident error or the statistical independence required by equation 3. The release manifest must therefore retain the full body of raw physical evidence needed for an independent audit, not just the final verdict.

The adjudicator ultimately signs one of the three decisions summarized in table 4. A conditional verdict requires its own independently evidenced restricted envelope; an unresolved gap in the nominal argument cannot be relabeled as an operational constraint. No verdict ever moves the proposal boundary. Under both UNCOND and COND, learned policies only propose candidate actions while the deterministic safety enforcer independently admits or blocks every control command, as principle 3 requires. The release verdict sets the physical envelope that the permission path enforces; it never grants learned policies unchecked authority.

Each verdict in table 4 is signed against the physical state of the machine at the moment of evaluation. The right-hand column of the table therefore pairs every operational grant with an explicit expiry or refusal trigger, because a brake lining or sensor mount that passed inspection before commissioning does not stay in its commissioning state. As the physical plant wears and drifts over time, a release verdict holds only while runtime checks, scheduled measurements, and enforced site procedures continue to verify its standing premises.

16.5 The Verdict’s Standing Conditions

A signed release can lapse without any change to the underlying software. Brake linings wear down, camera brackets shift under continuous vibration, or warehouse traffic polishes concrete lanes until traction drops. When these physical premises drift, the stopping claim certified by the adjudicator no longer holds. Standing conditions bind the operational verdict to these physical baselines by defining an explicit observation cadence for each premise alongside deterministic fallback responses when a check fails. Because failure modes originate in hardware and environment, their observation schedules must follow physical degradation mechanisms rather than software sprint schedules. An assurance case maintained this way, its validity updated throughout operating life as monitored evidence shifts, is what the safety literature calls a dynamic safety case (Denney et al. 2015).

16.5.1 Degradation, drift, and observation cadence

None of the three verdicts is a permanent grant. Mechanical linkages develop backlash, friction surfaces wear, optical lenses collect dust, and operating environments change. Operating under either an unconditional (UNCOND) or conditional (COND) verdict therefore carries continuous obligations for the life of the deployment. Notebook 16.1 shows how little wear the stopping claim tolerates. A credible deceleration 31 percent below the assumed 2 m/s² spends the whole partial spare, and a smaller loss spends the reserve that remains once admission work is charged, leaving no allowance for perception latency or traction variation. A lining that wears that far is discovered at a rack end as an overrun unless the degradation first trips a standing condition, such as a scheduled loaded deceleration trial or an automated pre-shift brake diagnostic, before the machine is cleared to enter the aisle. Every operational obligation pairs a physical premise with an automated detector and an immediate mitigation, establishing a standing condition tied to the release verdict.

Sensor mounting drift can silently invalidate localization while every camera frame stays sharp and fresh. Daily thermal cycling and machine vibration alter extrinsic sensor geometry, and software rollback cannot realign a physically displaced bracket. The safety case must therefore require qualified online drift detection or scheduled physical recalibrations, setting inspection intervals from the underlying mechanical wear rates. A cryptographic checksum confirms only that the stored calibration file matches its build artifact; it gives no evidence that the physical camera still sits at that modeled pose.

A release verdict depends on physical premises with widely differing observation schedules. For instance, the mobile manipulator’s optimistic all-weight traction screen requires a floor friction coefficient of at least 0.204; the braking claim additionally requires qualified loaded wheel forces, drive conditions, and stopping traces. Dry concrete exceeds this threshold by a wide margin, at roughly 0.60, but a baseline site survey conducted before commissioning cannot guarantee surface conditions against fluid spills or traffic polishing over time. The safety case must therefore specify routine floor maintenance schedules alongside strict exclusion rules whenever surface contamination occurs, supplemented by any onboard traction monitoring. Similarly, the arm’s contact-force sensors require periodic recalibration against reference loads. Every physical premise must be rechecked continuously or periodically at a cadence matched to how fast it degrades. This is the fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), applied over time. The verdict grants permission contingent on operating conditions, and that permission endures only while the evidence supporting each premise remains valid.

Every runtime-monitored premise requires a dedicated detector and a pre-allocated fallback response; conversely, premises governed by facility maintenance require accountable operators and enforceable exclusion rules. If a safety case credits a navigation-camera dispatch age of 51.6 ms, the runtime monitor must measure frame arrival latency against hardware clocks, accounting for sensor readout and transport delays separately. Forwarding an unverified metadata timestamp does not confirm that the frame is fresh. Without hardware-synchronized event timestamps, unverified inputs quietly turn safety premises into ungrounded assumptions. Different premises demand different sampling frequencies. The tote-rack load, bounded by its 50 kg mechanical payload limit, can be checked at each load-changing event if access controls exclude unrecorded loading and the check completes before the changed load receives motion authority. Conversely, base velocity, position tracking, and action lease validity must be evaluated deterministically on every 1 ms safety-enforcer tick.

16.5.2 The envelope trichotomy and epistemic gaps

A naive binary health check assumes normal operation whenever no explicit error flag is asserted, mistaking a dead sensor or a diverged filter for safe clearance. Checking monitored premises at runtime instead exposes three states of envelope membership: known-true, known-false, and unknown. Membership is known-true when authenticated telemetry and hardware-referenced timestamps confirm that the plant’s bounded state-uncertainty set remains strictly inside certified envelope boundaries. Estimator convergence or small covariance matrices alone do not prove containment if model assumptions have broken down. When measured telemetry reveals a physical boundary violation, such as base tracking error exceeding the 40 mm bound reserved within the stopping envelope, the state is known-false. Finally, when an optical encoder loses serial communication, a camera frame fails its cyclic redundancy check (CRC), or an extended Kalman filter diverges, the system enters an epistemic gap. In this unknown state, the controller cannot tell whether the physical machine is safely positioned or breaching clearance margins.

When telemetry supporting active motion becomes unknown or known-false, the safety enforcer revokes trajectory tracking and executes the pre-certified fallback procedure for the plant’s dynamic state. This runtime intervention differs from rejecting an invalid future trajectory proposal, because an already active trajectory chunk can continue executing only while its supporting sensor evidence, physical bounds, and lease duration remain intact. The system’s flight recorder must log whether revocation stemmed from unobservable state telemetry or an active boundary breach, while the hardware supplies enough reserve energy and sensing capability to bring the plant to a controlled stop or hold.

Definition 16.3: Envelope trichotomy

Envelope trichotomy is the three-state epistemic classification of cyber-physical system health relative to its signed operating envelope: known-true (qualified state-uncertainty set contained inside the supported envelope), known-false (verified boundary violation triggering deterministic hardware refusal), and unknown (state uncertainty caused by sensor dropout, frame corruption, or estimator divergence).

  1. Significance: An unknown state is not evidence of a boundary breach, yet neither condition permits continued tracking. The permission decision is refusal in both unknown and known-false states; the logged cause remains distinct and the response depends on the plant’s validated stop or hold capability.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a binary boolean status check (is_safe == true), the envelope trichotomy separates sensor blindness from active violations, ensuring telemetry loss is never mistaken for safe clearance.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a stored command as indefinitely valid after communication fails. An active chunk may continue only within its own still-valid evidence and lease; otherwise the assessed fallback must take over.

The same three-state distinction applies to verification test records. A test trial marked UNOBSERVABLE or INVALID during acceptance testing (Four verdicts and the invalid-test rule) cannot establish the claim its required witnesses or test conditions failed to resolve. It leaves a gap in that coverage; other valid records remain evidence only for the conditions and claims they establish. Verified PASS records support the claims covered by their conditions and witnesses. A valid FAIL contradicts the claim tested under those conditions and withdraws any authorization that relies on it.

16.5.3 Blind travel, kinetic consequences, and cycle limits

When telemetry fails or sensors black out, a machine traveling without sensory feedback moves blind; it must execute a deterministic fallback stop before its forward travel consumes the remaining spatial clearance. How fast that clearance vanishes depends directly on vehicle speed, contact geometry, and available actuator authority. Sizing the response therefore requires calculating the kinetic energy and blind travel accumulated during each millisecond of delay, and matching each sensor loss to a pre-certified fallback path.

How the system responds when a premise cannot be checked must follow from the physics of unmonitored motion rather than from arbitrary severity categories. When the mobile manipulator traverses an aisle at 1.3 m/s with its tote rack fully loaded, its 368 kg mass carries 311 J of kinetic energy. If its primary lidar stops updating, every millisecond of blind travel carries the base 1.3 mm closer to potential obstacles. The safety case pre-certifies three possible fallback paths, two of which apply only when the signed release names them in advance. The first is a degraded mode that permits continued motion while diverse redundant sensors keep state uncertainty bounded, for example throttling the aisle speed to 0.65 m/s (half the aisle speed, a chosen value) while secondary sensors watch the path. The second is a switch to a restricted limit set that a signed COND case pre-validated for that exact sensor loss, if the release includes one. The runtime only selects that profile; it does not create a new verdict. Dead reckoning and wheel odometry may retain qualified position bounds over a brief window, but cannot verify obstacle clearance on their own. The third option, the hardware STOP response, handles complete loss of observability by executing a configured drive and brake sequence. Stopping the vehicle within available clearance margins requires calibrated response timing, sufficient drive traction, and known brake deceleration.
