Action leases bound how long an admitted trajectory chunk may run without renewal. Checking this lease on every controller tick does not require re-measuring every long-term site condition. Instead, the runtime permission path checks fresh local sensor telemetry alongside the signed validity windows of slower site inspections and maintenance records. If the proposer stalls or its communication drops, task throughput suffers, but safety holds as long as the enforcer and its fallback path remain decoupled, responsive, and intact.

Any standing condition governing physical motion requires an enforcement path with a measured detection and response budget. In this book’s architecture, an independent safety enforcer checks trajectory proposals and monitored premises before issuing actuator permissions, re-evaluating system state on every 1 ms control tick. A subsystem failure reaches this enforcer along one of two paths: a silent proposer that stops transmitting is caught only when its existing action lease expires, whereas an actively monitored hardware fault raises an immediate interrupt flag. Because detection delays differ between these paths, so does the distance the base travels blind (notebook 16.2).

The lease dominates: a silent proposer reaches brake onset in 82 ms, a flagged premise in 22 ms.

Napkin Math 16.2: The silent-proposer window
Problem: The base runs the aisle at 1.3 m/s. How far does it travel before brake onset when the proposer falls silent, how far when a monitored premise fails and its flag reaches the enforcer at once, and how late may that flag arrive before monitoring stops the base no sooner than the lease would?

Variables:

  • Aisle speed \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.3 m/s (Chosen), so 1.3 mm of travel per millisecond
  • Chunk lease 60 ms and permission tick 1 ms (Chosen); bus transfer 1 ms and brake onset 20 ms (Illustrative)
  • Flag latency \(\FailureFlagLatency\) from the physical failure to a flag the enforcer can read (Unknown; the detector’s qualification must bound it)

Math:

  1. Silent proposer: 60 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 20 ms \(=\) 82 ms, and 1.3 m/s \(\times\) 82 ms \(=\) 106.6 mm.
  2. Monitored premise, immediate flag: 1 ms \(+\) 1 ms \(+\) 20 ms \(=\) 22 ms, and 1.3 m/s \(\times\) 22 ms \(=\) 28.6 mm.
  3. Crossover: \(\FailureFlagLatency +\) 22 ms \(=\) 82 ms at \(\FailureFlagLatency =\) 60 ms, the length of the lease itself.

Result: Monitoring can save up to 78 mm of travel, but only for a flag that arrives within 60 ms of the failure; a slower flag stops the base no sooner than waiting out the lease.

Systems insight: The 22 ms local subtotal is not a complete detection-to-stop time; it reflects only controller cycle delay, communication transfer, and physical brake engagement. The decisive term governing whether an active monitor preserves physical clearance is \(\FailureFlagLatency\), the hardware detection latency. If an architectural budget fails to bound \(\FailureFlagLatency\), the safety case cannot claim proactive protection. Every millisecond spent detecting a fault carries the loaded vehicle another 1.3 mm toward whatever the failed premise was protecting against.

When an operating premise transitions to unknown or known-false, the enforcer revokes trajectory tracking and commands the appropriate rung of the fallback ladder, and the signed safety case must specify both this fallback selection and its measured timing. For the mobile base, the certified stopping sequence uses controlled, powered deceleration to bring the wheels to rest before applying electromechanical holding brakes. Because digital controller logic and brake-coil hold-up capacitors cannot deliver drive motor torque, the safety case must confirm drive energy, feedback sensing, and inverter continuity across every fault condition expected to use this active deceleration profile. By contrast, a raw torque-inhibit or free-wheeling cutoff is a different physical response; it cannot inherit stopping-distance guarantees calculated for controlled electrical braking.

Entering an unknown or known-false state also triggers local black-box logging. The onboard recorder must preserve the telemetry window leading up to refusal alongside the execution of the fallback trajectory, even if the initiating fault cuts external networking or primary bus power. These records are essential for root-cause investigation, but writing them to nonvolatile storage must never delay the hard real-time safety stop; section 16.9.5 sizes the capture path.

On longer timescales, even when every runtime heartbeat succeeds, the release verdict carries an operational cycle and inspection budget.7 Operating hours and mechanical fatigue wear components in ways that high-frequency telemetry cannot observe directly (such as surface pitting on gear teeth, elastomer seal stiffening, or fastener preload relaxation). Once this cycle budget expires, the release case must be re-adjudicated before autonomous motion can resume. Across every timescale, a deployment verdict remains valid only while its supporting standing conditions remain demonstrably known-true, checked at intervals matched to physical wear rates and backed by pre-certified fallback stops with measured latencies. Claims, verdicts, and revocation conditions are now defined. The six claims of the warehouse mobile manipulator can be adjudicated against the physical evidence assembled across this book.

7 Mechanical Fatigue Budget: A cumulative-damage model such as Miner’s rule (\(\sum_i \AppliedLoadCycleCount_i/\FatigueLifeCycleCount_i\)) (Miner 1945) can organize a measured load spectrum against component-specific fatigue data; it is an approximation, not a universal failure deadline. The assessed loads, uncertainty, inspection evidence, and stopping consequences determine any signed payload derating and cycle limit. An expired limit revokes the applicable release case rather than silently extending service.

Miner, Milton A. 1945. “Cumulative Damage in Fatigue.” Journal of Applied Mechanics 12 (3): A159–64. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.4009458.

16.6 Adjudicating the Safety Case

An algebraically exact stopping equation or contact-force bound still fails to protect a machine if the physical premise beneath its warrant has never been measured on hardware. Adjudicating a safety release case requires checking each warrant against the qualified measurements, justified analysis, or validated operating restrictions that support it. For the warehouse mobile manipulator, the adjudicator takes each of the six claims of table 2 in turn, names the physical premise under its warrant, and grades the evidence behind that premise. This isolates the exact boundary where mathematical deduction ends and physical measurement must take over.

The door-opening branch of C1 shows the procedure in full. The contact enforcement record supplies the branch’s underlying mechanics (Intentional contact and in-contact energy barriers). At the guarded approach velocity of 0.03 m/s, equation 12.11 yields an impact force at physical braking onset of 39 N, well below the 100 N latch limit. From there, equation 12.12 leaves the latch plate with only 0.15 mm of additional allowable compression. Bringing the arm to rest within this narrow mechanical travel requires a deceleration of approximately 3 m/s² over a brief 10 ms stopping window. By contrast, at the unconstrained approach velocity of 0.10 m/s, the force at braking onset rises to 95 N, and avoiding structural damage demands a deceleration of 400 m/s², more than an order of magnitude above standard gravity. While this kinematic calculation specifies the stopping performance required for release, mathematical derivation alone cannot prove that the physical arm actuators can deliver it. Consequently, the operating envelope (table 1) excludes the high-speed approach. Raising approach speeds sharply escalates contact braking demands, whereas the system’s training demonstrations offer zero empirical evidence for safe high-speed contact (Choosing a tractable coverage table).

Raising the approach from 0.03 m/s to 0.10 m/s multiplies the arm stop needed 136-fold.

Neither result is in doubt as arithmetic; the algebraic deduction is exact. Formal engineering release, however, cannot rest on closed-form kinematic models alone. The door branch fails authorization because its safety warrant depends on an unverified physical premise. Whether the physical manipulator, carrying its link momentum, payload mass, and reflected rotor inertia, can actually deliver 3 m/s² of deceleration within a fraction of a millimeter depends on transient joint torque limits, motor electrical time constants, and structural stiffness. Without calibrated hardware traces measuring these dynamic properties under load, this mechanical premise remains unknown, and the adjudicator must refuse the claim.

The remaining claim branches fail on similar grounds:

  • Base braking corridor (C1): The base stopping budget of 969.0 mm omits dispatch-to-admission delay and rests on illustrative values for braking deceleration, brake onset, path-tracking error, and localization uncertainty. Its remaining margin of 131.0 mm can be exhausted by the illustrative deceleration shortfall in step 4 of notebook 16.1, and step 5 leaves still less once admission work is charged.
  • Object handover (C1): The mug handover branch fails on three separate premises. First, the calculated contact force falls below the criterion only for an unconstrained, free-moving hand, and it comes from a standardized biomechanical body model rather than a force measurement of this contact. Second, the evidence packet does not show that the coworker’s hand cannot be trapped against a rigid fixture, so the non-clamping geometry is unproven. Third, perceptual grounding remains unverified: a proposal that mistakes a clear glass jar for the target mug satisfies every geometric affordance check, and no trace in the runtime logs disambiguates the object.
  • Enforcer timing (C2): The timing warrant for the runtime safety enforcer assumes a worst-case execution time (WCET) of 250 μs against an unloaded baseline of 135 μs and a 400 μs deadline. The WCET is declared without architectural analysis and loaded stress evidence supporting it under peak memory bus saturation.
  • Traction and environmental state (C3): Claim C3 requires a qualified friction floor across every authorized aisle; 0.204 is only the optimistic all-weight traction screen, not a sufficient loaded stopping qualification. A clean concrete floor can be validated through baseline skid testing, but the onboard cameras cannot reliably distinguish dry concrete from a thin film of transparent oil, and no sensor on the machine can exclude liquid contamination ahead of the braking contact.
  • Perception pipeline latency (C4): The 51.6 ms dispatch age combines nominal datasheet readout times with uncharacterized pipeline delays rather than verified end-to-end telemetry.
  • Permission independence (C5): The placement record’s loaded test report, which would show the enforcer’s maxima under the proposer’s worst interference and test the common causes of Two Paths and the Resources They Share, is described in this book but not produced. Without it, the credited enforcement isolation is unsubstantiated; even supporting isolation records would not by themselves establish statistical independence of every failure event.
  • Authority transfer (C6): The argument cites PASS fault records for authority transitions A1 through A4, and no such records exist yet. Local operation could drop the remote-channel cases, but not the local ones.

Because every claim rests on at least one unverified physical premise, the adjudicator must render a verdict of REFUSE. This refusal does not imply that the robot is inherently dangerous. The book’s supporting inputs are illustrative models rather than empirical validation logs, so the verdict is illustrative as well; it models how release is adjudicated and shows why ungrounded claims cannot license physical deployment. The fourth law, permission must be earned, requires an operational restriction wherever the available evidence cannot support a premise. Here, every branch contains an unverified premise. A principled refusal must therefore specify the exact measurements required to turn an unverified assumption into an accepted warrant. Table 5 lists these evidentiary requirements, naming the physical premise each measurement settles and the operational restriction a supporting result would lift.

↩︎ The four bedrock laws, the fourth of which holds that permission must be earned, are established in The Four Bedrock Laws.

Neural network inference latency primarily governs system availability rather than baseline safety. An inference overrun will not violate physical containment so long as the independent hardware safety path and powered fallback controllers remain operational; however, repeated deadline misses will degrade throughput and stall task progress. Conversely, resource contention across shared memory, PCIe buses, or CPU caches that delays the runtime enforcer or starves its sensor feeds represents a direct safety failure under claims C2 and C5. Finally, while local autonomous operation can safely omit validation trials for unused remote teleoperation links, deployment still requires verified software placement records, comprehensive common-cause fault coverage, and passing test records for every local authority transition.

16.6.1 Worked restricted service with stipulated evidence

Without calibrated hardware measurements, the mobile manipulator remains locked out under a REFUSE verdict. To see how an engineering organization moves from initial refusal to conditional deployment without relaxing its structural safety limits, consider a stipulated release case in which an engineering packet supplies the measured traces specified below. (The uncalibrated evidence currently available in this book continues to dictate REFUSE.)

The target application is restricted inspected-route mug delivery. The robot retrieves a declared mug from a singulated feeder and traverses a designated, surveyed warehouse aisle at a velocity capped at 1 m/s. It then pauses the mobile base to open an equipment cage latch using the guarded contact trajectory and hands the mug to a consenting human coworker under the accept_item primitive at 0.10 m/s. Operational authority remains strictly local. The feeder mechanism physically prevents accidental object substitutions, recognizing that singulation alone cannot authenticate identity. Similarly, strict access controls prevent personnel from stepping into the robot’s active stopping corridor during transit.

↪ The difference between a coworker who consents to a machine’s risk, as this one does, and people who bear it without choosing, is developed in Who Bears the Risk.

For claim C1, the safety case stipulates empirical stopping traces under full payload mass, measured tracking and localization variance, dynamic latch-stopping performance, and instrumented handover contact forces that satisfy the bounded thresholds in table 5. For claims C2 and C5, the case stipulates independent hardware fault-injection logs and worst-case execution timing traces gathered under peak synthetic computational load. These records must account for common-cause hardware failures, support the enforcer’s permission deadlines and actuator reaction latencies, separate controlled motor deceleration from passive mechanical braking at rest, and confirm isolated drive-energy cutoff and sensor integrity under all credited fault modes.

For claim C4, the case stipulates synchronized end-to-end hardware timestamp telemetry, bounded clock skew, and a verified upper bound on dispatch-to-admission latency. This latency must be deducted from the dynamic stopping distance budget so that a strictly positive safety margin remains; a theoretical kinematic velocity ceiling cannot authorize transit speeds on its own. Because sensor data ages continuously throughout execution, the chunk lease starts only when a plan is admitted, never when it is computed. The qualification packet must also check the time between admitted plans against the lease renewal deadline rather than assuming that nominal policy inference frequency guarantees admission. For claim C6, the packet stipulates verified PASS audit records across every state transition exercised by the local autonomous executive, while disabling the unverified remote teleoperation interface entirely.

Claim C3 requires substantially more than visual inspection or reduced transit velocity. The case stipulates a qualified site friction survey showing a friction coefficient of at least 0.12 across the surveyed transit corridor, reinforced by active cleaning protocols, chemical containment barriers, and physical access gates that maintain floor integrity throughout the authorized inspection interval. Stopping trials at maximum payload must confirm deceleration on this specific friction surface with the loaded wheel distribution and drive settings of the signed configuration. The complete restricted stopping distance, including the verified dispatch-to-admission delay bound and a positive safety margin, must fit inside the surveyed physical clearance with a positive reserve, the same accounting as step 5 of notebook 16.1. The operating velocity of 1 m/s sits below the partial ceiling of 1.23 m/s, but that comparison is only an initial feasibility filter. The active transit permit is revoked immediately upon any reported spill, expired maintenance inspection, or perimeter breach. If unmonitored oil or other fluid can enter the corridor during the deployment window, the stipulation is invalidated and the verdict reverts to REFUSE. A lower speed cannot repair that premise, because the restricted speed was sized for the inspected friction floor of 0.12, not for a film near 0.05 that removes most of it.

The task capability packet is similarly stipulated. Before evaluation begins, the facility operator defines binding acceptance criteria in the verification plan: a minimum mission completion rate, an upper bound on end-to-end cycle time, and a maximum allowable operator intervention rate, accompanied by sample-size requirements, confidence intervals, and statistical decision rules. A successful mission requires maintaining object identity and physical integrity from retrieval through coworker handover. Cycle time accounting includes transient safety stops, crossing delays, and autonomous replanning; human rescues and aborted deliveries are tracked as explicit mission failures. The safety case stipulates that closed-loop trials of this exact restricted configuration satisfy all three benchmarks. The qualification dossier separates raw neural policy autonomy, system-level task success, and safety interventions into independent metrics, the separation What a Physical Success Rate Measures drew between the policy’s own completions and the completions that protection made possible. Minimal-intervention safety filtering may run during nominal task execution; its clamps are counted separately, so they neither mask the policy’s shortcomings nor count as safety violations.

With these stipulated evidence packets in hand, the adjudicator can sign a verdict of COND (conditional release) for restricted inspected-route mug delivery. The physical records support claims C1 through C6 solely within the restricted operating envelope, while the task evaluation shows acceptable operational utility under identical constraints. The adjudicator binds both the physical safety dossier and the capability evaluation to the release manifest, leaving full-speed autonomous transit and remote teleoperation unauthorized. The result is a conditional safety case, not a blanket deployment permit. Invalidating any underlying assumption or breaching a boundary condition immediately returns the verdict to REFUSE; conversely, gathering qualified operational telemetry and physical measurements over time can justify expanding the envelope toward full-scale release.

Checkpoint 16.2: Refusal and the path to conditional release

Before approving a conditional release of the machine, verify your understanding of how a premise, rather than a calculation, decides a claim:

16.7 Adversarial Review

Confirmation bias steers an engineering team’s data collection toward nominal conditions where learned models succeed, leaving edge-case hazards untested until live operation exposes them. Suppose the measurements listed in table 5 arrive and, as in the stipulated branch, appear to support a conditional (COND) authorization for the mobile manipulator. Before signing, the safety adjudicator submits the safety case to an independent adversarial review tasked with falsifying its warrants. Rather than ratifying a brief, the reviewer actively searches for unsupported warrants, uncharacterized physical transitions, and environmental edge cases that violate the release contract. If an argument collapses on paper when an adversary probes for physical violations, the machine cannot be expected to withstand the hazards of live service.

The review interrogates every evidentiary node in the argument tree using four structured probes. The first challenges empirical sufficiency: whether the physical trials reached the extremes of the operating envelope. Stopping trials with the loaded base on dry concrete, at a friction coefficient near 0.60, support the warrant that the base achieves its certified deceleration under ideal conditions. The restricted operational mode, however, admits facility floors that the site’s inspection regime certifies only down to 0.12, such as an aisle polished smooth by traffic. In those zones, the base may run at the restricted velocity 1 m/s, below the 1.23 m/s ceiling. If the trial set contains no loaded stops on such degraded floors at that speed, the warrant for the restricted mode is unsupported. A coverage audit finds on paper the gap that unvetted service would otherwise find against a warehouse rack. The reviewer also checks whether validation trials covered transient execution states across an A/B partition swap, when a newly flashed software bank takes over from the incumbent one (section 16.9.1). The same check applies to cold-start sensor characteristics before cameras and inertial measurement units reach thermal equilibrium. It applies as well to the operating boundaries of canary units, the small subset of the fleet that first runs a candidate policy (section 16.9.2). Finally, the reviewer asks whether site procedures actually keep fluid films out of the corridor throughout the authorized interval, since listing oil outside the envelope does not exclude it from the floor.

The second probe examines domain transfer, targeting claims where synthetic simulation substitutes for direct physical measurement. A vision-based human detector may achieve high recall across synthetic, ray-traced warehouse environments, yet fail on physical hardware when encountering coworkers partially obscured by stretch-wrapped pallets whose specular reflections blind the camera’s auto-exposure algorithm. Benchmark recall on curated offline datasets is an indirect proxy metric that scores isolated frames rather than the closed-loop physical causal chain running from visual detection to an arrested base. Likewise, a simulation that omits linkage compliance or fieldbus jitter cannot establish tolerance to those physical mechanisms. The reviewer demands empirical evidence from paired hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) trials on target SoC devices connected to chassis dynamometers8 and physical bus emulators under real facility illumination, dust, and reflective geometry.

8 Chassis Dynamometer: Powered rollers under the drive wheels absorb or supply torque, so the base can run at aisle speed on a bench while its drive and braking torques are measured. The rollers, not the floor, set the traction. Dynamometer stops can therefore qualify drive and brake response but say nothing about the friction premise of C3.

The third probe attacks structural independence, asking whether the learned model and safety enforcer share hardware resources whose contention could delay sensor delivery or actuation cutoff. On modern robotics edge platforms where neural inference engines and real-time safety enforcers share a single system-on-chip die, internal crossbar interconnects, shared caches, and DRAM memory controllers, unconstrained direct memory access (DMA) transfers from large model forward passes can saturate memory channels. This memory contention induces tail latency spikes that starve deterministic safety enforcer execution ticks, directly invalidating the 51.6 ms observation-age chain of C4. Stress tests and hardware timing analysis under peak synthetic compute and bus loads must establish the worst-case interference bound; unit tests on an otherwise idle bus provide zero evidence of non-interference. The reviewer also checks whether writing frozen edge telemetry ring buffers to non-volatile storage competes with real-time fieldbus DMA buffers across the internal bus fabric. The reviewer inspects watchdog reset circuits for single points of failure and verifies whether the supercapacitor power hold-up circuit supplies enough energy to flush in-flight telemetry buffers to persistent flash when an emergency stop opens the main DC bus contactors.

The fourth probe identifies unmonitored state drift. Mechanical wear alters gearbox compliance9 and disrupts the dynamic relationship between commanded motor torque and physical joint motion. The reviewer therefore demands paired wear-state braking and contact traces before accepting the arm-stop premise of the door branch of C1 (section 16.6). A static motor-current threshold alone cannot prove that a mechanically worn drive train remains inside certified force and stopping limits. The reviewer also checks whether over-the-air updates preserve the persistent hardware calibration partition intact across schema migrations, or whether an OS reflash can silently reset camera extrinsics and joint zeroes to unverified factory defaults. Finally, the probe checks whether thermal bracket motion stays inside the localization allowance; fresh timestamps and unchanged calibration files on disk cannot establish that physical fact.

9 Strain-Wave Gear Wear: The arm’s joints use strain-wave reducers (Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture), and in service their flexspline teeth and wave-generator bearing wear. Wear raises lost motion and torsional hysteresis, which adds phase lag between motor and link, so the arm begins to decelerate later and its first contact peak rises.

Reviewers turn these vulnerabilities into concrete tests by constructing explicit defeaters for each warrant. The hardest failure modes to catch combine minor anomalies that each stay inside their own tolerance bands, the compound faults that Compound faults and coupled disturbances derives from shared causes. Consider the mobile manipulator’s navigation camera operating with three simultaneous sub-threshold degradations. A light film of dust coats its lens. A mount whose fasteners relaxed within tolerance vibrates as the base crosses floor joints. An interconnect harness flexes at those joints and elevates the bit-error rate across its serializer/deserializer (SerDes) link. Each individual check reads normal. The optical transmission score exceeds its 85 percent alarm threshold. The mount deflection registers below its 0.15-degree hardware fault limit. SerDes cyclic redundancy check error counters remain below their warning tripwire. Their timing effects remain contained only if the independent permission path strictly enforces the event-specific observation-age and clock-uncertainty checks of The Observation Contract and retains its validated response time. Any hardware or bus interference that defeats that deterministic path is a safety violation under C5, not a benign availability-only event.

The same anomalies compound within spatial estimation. Dimmed contrast reduces tracked visual features, mount vibration adds motion blur and persistent pose bias, and frame drops extend intervals of uncorrected dead reckoning. A systematic pose bias can quietly erode the localization allowance without forcing estimated filter covariances or innovation residuals to breach trip thresholds. The release reviewer therefore challenges the combined error against the qualified uncertainty bound, rather than inferring containment from individually passing scalar diagnostics. A breach of the combined bound consumes part of the stopping reserve and invalidates the release warrant; exhausting the entire stopping margin requires a quantified combined-error scenario. Statistical residual monitoring protects against only the specific faults its qualification shows it can detect, leaving site operational restrictions or independent physical checks to cover the remaining biases.

The review also audits the execution integrity of the permission path, checking whether inference loops or background services can bypass, corrupt, or starve the enforcer or human overrides, the latter covered by claim C6 of table 2. Below the proposal boundary, both safety monitors and manual overrides maintain non-preemptible execution priority over learned models. The reviewer asks concrete systems questions: whether a runaway policy thread can exhaust shared memory channels, whether an accelerator-runtime exception can lock a kernel spinlock, and whether a telemetry logging burst can brown out a regulated power rail. The reviewer accepts permission path independence, and the held-up power rail that carries it on the mobile manipulator, only after reviewing physical fault-injection trials. These trials must show survival under bus saturation, active feed removal, and an abruptly killed proposer task (fault F1), and they must appear in the signed fault records, because C2 claims only the faults the permission path demonstrably survives. A user-space implementation without qualified scheduling, resource isolation, and response mechanisms cannot supply the justified execution bound and fault isolation the reviewer requires.

When an adversarial probe succeeds, the case cannot be salvaged by editing claim descriptions or inserting arbitrary safety margins. The broken warrant is traced to its root systems failure (an uncharacterized physical boundary, an inadequate dataset, or an unmodeled bus latency), and the defect returns to the owning engineering subsystem. For example, a motor-current observer that fails under gearbox compliance returns to the mechanical specification as wear limits and runtime compliance estimation, while bus contention that delays the enforcer returns to the compute architecture as memory partitioning and real-time scheduling. The case returns to the engineering team with its broken warrants named, and only updated boundaries, re-derived stopping distances, and renewed verification reopen formal adjudication.

Adversarial review must also recur periodically, because a case accepted once can erode in service without any single discrete event forcing a new audit. The normalization of deviance names that operational erosion, in which anomalies that cause no immediate harm come to be treated as acceptable operating noise rather than as evidence against a warrant (Leveson 2011; Vaughan 1996) (war story 16.1).10 On the mobile manipulator, this erosion unfolds silently across months of fleet operation. Localization residuals creep toward the edge of their tolerance. Observation-age watchdog trips are dismissed as nuisance stops. Stopping distances end slightly longer than the initial trials did. Enforcer trips are cleared on the floor without a trace review. When thresholds are widened, margins trimmed for throughput, and alarms suppressed because nothing has yet gone wrong, the gap between the signed envelope and the machine’s actual physical behavior widens until a routine disturbance exhausts what remains. The defense is to fix each watched quantity’s threshold and response before service begins, as the safety performance indicators of section 16.9.5 do, so that a run of harmless readings cannot move it.

Leveson, Nancy. 2011. Engineering a Safer World: Systems Thinking Applied to Safety. MIT Press.

10 Normalization of Deviance: The sociologist Diane Vaughan coined the term in her study of the Challenger launch decision (Vaughan 1996). It names a process rather than a decision, in which each anomaly that caused no harm widened what the next review accepted as normal.

Vaughan, Diane. 1996. The Challenger Launch Decision: Risky Technology, Culture, and Deviance at NASA. University of Chicago Press.

War Story 16.1: Challenger and experience limits (1986)
Context: The Space Shuttle’s solid rocket motors were assembled from cylindrical steel segments whose field joints were sealed by primary and secondary O-rings (Presidential Commission on the Space Shuttle Challenger Accident 1986). No learned model appears in this chain. The adjudication failure it records is the exact failure this chapter’s verdicts exist to prevent: a launch approved for conditions that the evidence did not cover.

Mechanism: The ambient temperature at launch was \(36^\circ\text{F}\), 15 degrees colder than any previous launch. O-ring resiliency falls with temperature, and a cold ring returns to shape too slowly to follow the joint gap as ignition pressure opens it. On the eve of launch, engineers at Morton Thiokol, the motor’s contractor, recommended against launching below \(53^\circ\text{F}\), the O-ring temperature of the coldest previous flight, whose joints had shown the worst blow-by to date. That limit was the outer edge of flight experience, not a qualified bound. NASA managers countered with a qualification temperature that applied to the propellant’s bulk temperature rather than to the joint seals.

Impact: A combustion gas leak through the right booster’s aft field joint, beginning at or shortly after ignition, weakened or penetrated the External Tank. The vehicle broke up about 73 seconds after launch, and all seven crew members died.

Response: The presidential Commission attributed the failure to “a faulty design unacceptably sensitive to a number of factors,” and temperature heads its list of those factors. It recommended that the joint be changed and that the new design be certified by tests “over the full range of operating conditions, including temperature.”

Systems lesson: Thiokol management reversed its engineers’ recommendation at the urging of NASA’s Marshall Space Flight Center. Thiokol’s vice president of engineering testified, “We had to prove to them that we weren’t ready.” The burden of proof had inverted: rather than requiring proof that the joint was safe inside its evidence, adjudicators demanded proof that it was unsafe outside the evidence, where no data existed. The Commission also found that O-ring erosion had recurred on earlier flights and that NASA and Thiokol “accepted escalating risk apparently because they ‘got away with it last time’” (Presidential Commission on the Space Shuttle Challenger Accident 1986), so the flight record itself had been read as evidence of safety. A release verdict keeps the burden where the envelope trichotomy puts it. A condition outside the evidence, such as a seal temperature below all flight experience, is unknown, and the verdict refuses it.

The Tempe collision analyzed in When the Responsibilities Fail Together rested on an unmeasured premise of the same kind as Challenger’s seal temperature. Its vehicle operator was the countermeasure the whole system leaned on, so the premise was that the operator was watching, and nothing measured whether that held. A release case that relies on such a premise must bind its operational response, whether an outright refusal or a restricted case, to verified physical limits, as claim C6 does for authority transfer. Even a case that survives this adversarial review has had its warrants tested only in principle while its verdict still lives on paper. The release manifest turns the surviving argument and the verdict it supports into a signed, tamper-evident record that the machine itself checks before it moves.

↩︎ The operator-monitoring failures that a human-takeover premise invites, from mode confusion to authority ambiguity, are established in Authority Handover Failure Modes.

16.8 The Release Manifest

A safety case that lives only in static documents or procedural sign-off sheets cannot stop a robot from booting with an unverified policy checkpoint or stale sensor calibrations. To make the verdict enforceable on hardware, the adjudicator’s decision is recorded in the release manifest, the fourteenth record in the book’s record chain. This signed, tamper-evident data structure binds every upstream engineering contract, the Goal Structuring Notation argument graph, the operating envelope limits, and live standing conditions to the adjudicator’s verdict by cryptographic digest. The target platform refuses physical motion whenever runtime binary images, silicon attestation measurements, or underlying qualification data diverge from these signed digests.11

11 Cryptographic Manifest and Silicon Attestation: One-time programmable electronic fuses (OTP/eFuse) store the adjudicator’s public verification key in silicon, preventing unauthorized software updates from replacing the root of trust. Secure bootstrap validates each early boot stage before handing over execution control (Arbaugh et al. 1997), while measured boot extends cryptographic measurements into Trusted Platform Module Platform Configuration Registers (TPM PCRs). The hardware verifier checks these measurements against the digests specified in the signed manifest; any unsigned or modified binary image fails attestation and never receives an operational permit.

Arbaugh, William A., David J. Farber, and Jonathan M. Smith. 1997. “A Secure and Reliable Bootstrap Architecture.” Proceedings of the 1997 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy, 65–71. https://doi.org/10.1109/SECPRI.1997.601317.

↪ The residual-claims register, the record that follows the release manifest and ties each premise it leaves open to an operational restriction, is developed in A residual-claims register.

The release manifest gathers the engineering contracts established throughout this book. Each upstream record defines an operating boundary or contributes empirical data that the safety case must check; a signature on an isolated contract does not validate the system as a whole. Instead, the manifest links reviewed snapshots of these records into a single argument graph:

  1. Physical plant and timing base: The limit record (The six elements of a limit record) documents torque, velocity, and thermal dissipation bounds alongside their qualification data. The proposer’s payload requirements (Information the proposer must supply) specify candidate action horizons that feed the handoff record rather than standing as an isolated contract. The handoff record (The Handoffs Matrix) establishes deterministic fieldbus jitter, cycle deadlines, and real-time communication premises.
  2. Data, policy, and behavioral bounds: The provenance record (The Dataset Schema) captures dataset calibrations, sensor configurations, and operating-domain coverage. The policy manifest (The Policy Manifest) binds frozen neural network weights to the empirical behavioral bounds proven for that candidate model. The evaluation record (The Evaluation Record) details closed-loop task success rates, human intervention tallies, and stress-tested operational conditions.
  3. Perception, belief, and planning: The observation contract (The Observation Contract) enforces observation freshness and multi-sensor synchronization windows. The belief record (The Spatial Memory Handoff and State Invariants) specifies geometric state estimates, state covariance, and validity domains. The intent lease (The Intent Handoff and Bounded Authority) records task objectives, spatial tolerances, and preemption conditions. The trajectory record (The Trajectory Record) encodes motion profiles, kinematic seam continuity, execution deadlines, and safe stopping continuations.
  4. Deterministic containment, execution, and verification: The enforcement record (The admitted command schema) logs the command stream admitted by runtime safety filters, cleanly separated from the signed enforcement contract that specifies barrier functions and fallback triggers. The placement record (The Placement Record) documents the qualified compute scheduling, memory isolation, and processor affinity. The authority record (The Authority Record) documents arbitration switches and fallback outcomes. The fault records (Fault Records for Release) index tested fault-injection scenarios alongside known coverage gaps.

Beyond linking these upstream contracts, the release manifest adds its own fields. It carries the root claim, the cryptographic digest of the Goal Structuring Notation argument graph, and binary hashes across all deployed software (spanning learned policy weights, the deterministic enforcer logic in both nominal and restricted modes, and the real-time operating system kernel). It also carries sensor calibration tables, the adjudicator’s digital signature, mandatory standing operational conditions, an accumulated lifecycle wear budget of operating hours and load cycles, and a calendar expiration timestamp. All evidence links must resolve to these exact reviewed record digests, organized hierarchically beneath a single evidence root structured as a Merkle hash tree (Merkle 1988). Each parent digest is computed from its children’s digests, so changing an evidence leaf changes the root that the manifest signs. Only when the hardware root of trust authenticates this complete cryptographic tree will it permit the motor power stages to energize. While traditional release notes or post hoc compliance certificates summarize safety after the fact, the release manifest is an active, machine-checked gate. The robot checks it before closed-loop control begins, and its runtime monitors revoke motor tracking within a bounded detection-and-response window if any underlying evidence or environmental condition expires.

Merkle, Ralph C. 1988. “A Digital Signature Based on a Conventional Encryption Function.” Advances in Cryptology — CRYPTO ’87, Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 293: 369–78. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-48184-2_32.

↪ Byte layout: The release manifest’s fields, types, and digests are given in Release manifest.

Figure 3 routes every release attempt through authentication and physical standing checks before an operating permit can issue. The release gate authenticates the manifest, binary images, and complete set of fault records before verifying current sensor calibration, mechanical maintenance thresholds, and operational design domain conditions. Only an exact cryptographic and physical match enables the plant’s certified startup sequence. Table 6 lists the gate result for each combination of signed case and standing check, including the single path by which stale calibration still reaches COND. A separately signed COND case can tolerate a named stale calibration only when its restricted envelope either locks out the motions that depend on that sensor, such as prohibiting base transit when the tote-rack scale is stale, or widens the uncertainty allowance and derates speed to cover worst-case calibration drift. During active motion, any breached condition commands an assessed safe fallback before any switch of software partition (the A/B banks of section 16.9.1) or electrical isolation.

↪ Protocol: The gate’s step-by-step authorization protocol is given in Release-gate protocol.

Each empirical evidence leaf in the assurance Merkle tree must identify its raw sensor trace, instrumentation hardware, calibration log, machine configuration, and automated test harness script. Analytical leaves retain their derivations and assumptions; procedural leaves retain the accountable procedure and its validation for the signed envelope. Measured latency profiles enter the safety case as empirical percentiles, whereas deterministic real-time execution guarantees demand the analytically justified upper bounds specified in Real-Time Command Transport and Bus Determinism. Similarly, collaborative object-handover criteria require load-cell time series recorded against standardized anthropomorphic surrogates across specified contact geometries. By cryptographically anchoring these primary records, an independent auditor or verification engine can reproduce the experimental test suite and pinpoint the exact operating boundaries where each safety claim ceases to hold.

The release manifest bounds the physical envelope and hardware configuration under which its verdict remains valid. Modifying a neural policy checkpoint, altering a real-time thread priority, updating a device driver, or replacing an actuator with an unverified component revision immediately invalidates the manifest, requiring a re-adjudicated safety case. On the warehouse mobile manipulator, for example, the manifest enforces signed wear thresholds on harmonic-drive backlash and electromagnetic brake lining thickness derived from stopping-distance and payload trials. Operating envelope tripwires trigger immediate refusal: on-board load cells detect tote-rack overloading beyond rated payload capacity, while facility procedures prevent operation on unmapped or unsurveyed floor surfaces (table 1). The manifest also encodes the isolated, energy-buffered power rail of the permission path as a standing invariant, running a diagnostic self-test on the hold-up capacitor bank before granting boot authorization. Whenever a runtime sensor detects that a state variable has breached a signed threshold, the hardware permission path interrupts closed-loop trajectory tracking within its certified detection-and-response deadline and brings the system to an assessed fallback state.

To preserve traceability across time, the manifest binds all operational telemetry directly to the exact hardware-software revision that generated it. High-resolution local ring buffers capture boundary excursions for post-incident diagnostics, whereas fleet-wide telemetry summaries reveal systematic mechanical drift across hundreds of operating hours. The curation pipelines examined in section 16.9.5 must preserve this cryptographic lineage whenever field data is promoted to support a later release case.

During daily operation, edge telemetry logs motor torque residuals, control-loop cycle times, and safety enforcer interventions for trend analysis, while the hardware permission path checks physical invariants on every 1 ms permission tick. If a routine maintenance check measures a stopping distance exceeding the signed clearance budget, or if a camera calibration certificate expires, the release verdict is revoked automatically. Re-flashing an older firmware image cannot compensate for worn mechanical friction pads. Unless an engineer deploys a separately signed restricted case (COND) that explicitly accommodates that degraded physical state, the controller executes an assessed emergency stop and locks out autonomous motion until certified maintenance and re-adjudication take place (table 6).

Once its powered stop has brought the base to rest, the warehouse mobile manipulator can reach a quiescent state only after the arm and payload are supported and the assessed holding brakes are engaged before drive power is removed. The base rests stably on the floor, but that support alone does not secure its gravity-loaded arm. For other morphologies, severing motor power is itself the hazard, because the machine collapses under gravity once torque is removed (perspective 16.1).

Systems Perspective 16.1: When removing torque causes the harm

Question: Which verdict applies when removing torque causes the harm?

A supported wheeled base at rest eliminates its translational kinetic hazard, though its arm and payload still require qualified support. In contrast, an upright bipedal humanoid retains substantial gravitational potential energy. While resting in a support gantry before standing up, a REFUSE verdict safely holds the robot in its mechanically supported state. Once balanced upright, however, revoking locomotion authority cannot simply de-energize the actuators without inducing an immediate dynamic fall; safe shutdown requires an active, validated arrest or controlled descent strategy.

  • Torque removal can cause a fall. An unsupported biped with backdrivable transmissions will collapse if motor currents drop to zero: torque removal leaves gravity-loaded joints without support (Actuation Authority), and an upright biped without active balance diverges like an inverted pendulum (Coupling the distance, lease, and supply budgets). Consequently, such physical plants cannot treat torque removal as a fail-safe stopping condition unless accompanied by certified passive catches, lock-up brakes, or an external tether.
  • The fall-arrest response is itself an actuation. Executing a controlled squat, brace, or step-down to bring the robot to rest is itself an active trajectory that requires validated sensorimotor claims, verified timing margins, and fault-tolerance guarantees. In this regime, executing a safe REFUSE action depends on the very control systems maintaining upright stability.
  • Wear revokes the running permit. Degradation in foot-sole rubber traction or increased backlash in planetary gearheads alters the dynamic margin required for emergency balancing. When telemetry signals that physical wear has crossed the threshold certified in the safety case, the runtime supervisor must revoke high-speed dynamic locomotion well before the safety-critical arrest maneuvers degrade below their qualifying safety margins.
Checkpoint 16.3: Deriving a verdict from your own measurements

Table 5 lists the measurements needed to support a release case but supplies no raw trial results. Make a copy with columns for value, source, provenance, and premise status, fill those columns with empirical data from hardware trials on your own robotic platform or an illustrative benchmark scenario, and evaluate the resulting release verdict:

A release manifest binds the verdict to an explicit, reviewed system configuration. Any later modification, whether retraining a perception head, tightening an actuator torque limit, or swapping a camera lens, breaks that closure and reopens some of the underlying claims. The question the rest of the chapter answers is which ones, and how the machine stays contained while the reopened claims are requalified.

16.9 Maintaining Release Through Updates

Continuing the stipulated warehouse branch, suppose the mobile manipulator’s restricted mug-delivery policy is retrained to reduce hesitation at the controlled pick station. Pushing that updated checkpoint directly to the fleet would invalidate the signed manifest and expose the machine to unverified bus loads and contact trajectories. Before the candidate neural policy can issue commands to physical actuators, it must pass back through the release decision. A signed safety case establishes which historical evidence remains valid, which hardware-in-the-loop tests must run again, and how the machine maintains physical containment throughout the update.

↩︎ Every updated checkpoint is a new policy whose earlier evaluations no longer apply, the rule established in Real-Robot Fine-Tuning and Residual Control that gives each one its own manifest and regression evidence.

16.9.1 Modular safety cases and over-the-air updates

Retraining a policy directly alters capability evidence, and neural modifications often introduce side effects across the shared hardware platform: increased neural accelerator memory bus traffic, altered sensor frame processing latency, or modified contact force trajectories. An engineer must trace these cross-subsystem dependencies before deciding which containment records remain valid. While over-the-air (OTA) updates swap out a named software payload, modular safety cases isolate dependencies by recording how each claim relies on specific evidence items and platform assumptions (Denney et al. 2015). Whenever an update alters an underlying dependency, the modular case shows exactly which safety claims are reopened.

The containment argument is the defense against physical harm. It binds together the plant model, physical sensor limits, worst-case execution budgets, and the independent permission path. Key engineering premises include the bounded tracking error tube \(\TrackingErrorBound\), the emergency stopping budget, the passivity barrier for physical contact, and hardware watchdog timing margins. Historical verification records for these containment claims remain valid only if the proposed update preserves the exact operating conditions under which they were measured. For example, replacing a backbone network with a higher-capacity model can increase memory bus contention or cause voltage droop on the power rail even if the deterministic safety-filter binary remains byte-for-byte identical. An unchanged enforcer binary does not imply an unchanged containment argument; its guarantees remain bounded by the validated dynamic envelope \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) and the system-level timing margins established during qualification.

↩︎ The tracking-error tube \(\TrackingErrorBound\) that the containment argument consumes as an invariant is established in The Tracker Handoff and Dynamic Reference Envelopes.

In parallel, the capability argument shows that the robot reliably fulfills its mission within its certified operational design domain. Candidate benchmarking, passive shadow traces, and geofenced closed-loop trials test whether a retrained policy reduces hesitation or improves task throughput. While the low-level enforcer can continuously clamp or reject invalid control proposals to maintain safety, an enforcer that constantly intervenes degrades the robot into an unusable, halting machine. Release engineering therefore evaluates capability and containment together. An update qualifies for fleet dispatch only when the candidate configuration delivers acceptable task performance without undermining the empirical premises supporting its containment envelope.

Modern physical AI platforms preserve configuration integrity during deployment through dual A/B OS partitioning. The update agent streams the candidate image onto the inactive storage bank (Bank B) while the incumbent controller continues executing from the active bank (Bank A). Activating the newly flashed partition requires an assessed stationary update state. The robot must be parked with its payload supported. Battery reserves must exceed with margin the energy needed for re-flashing and post-boot self-tests. The independent safety supervisor must hold all motor drives unpowered or mechanically locked so the robot cannot move during bootloader cutover. Hardware watchdogs govern the runtime decision to commit or abort this cutover (section 16.9.4). A single fleetwide signing key is a single point of compromise, because an attacker who extracts that key from one compromised endpoint could sign arbitrary firmware for every robot in the field. Update architectures therefore use role-separated signing structures where distinct root, targets, and release keys live on separate, hardware-isolated machines. The Update Framework (TUF) pioneered this design, and Uptane extended it to automotive and robotics systems where heterogeneous electronic control units require independently authenticated firmware images (Samuel et al. 2010; Kuppusamy et al. 2016).

Overwriting firmware must never destroy the physical calibration history of an individual robot. Persistent hardware calibration parameter retention separates machine-specific measurements from generic operating system binaries, preventing a calibration clashing failure. Camera intrinsic matrices (\(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix\)), extrinsic sensor transformations (\([\DirectionalCosineMatrix \mid \RigidTransformTranslation]\)), kinematic joint zero-point offsets, and motor torque constants (\(\MotorTorqueConstant\)) reflect the mechanical and electrical geometry of an individual machine and drift over time with vibration, thermal cycling, and wear. Storing these values in a dedicated, wear-leveled, non-volatile parameter partition keeps calibration intact when an OS image is flashed. When a firmware update introduces a revised parameter schema, the migration utility writes the transformed schema into a new record while preserving the legacy block, allowing direct rollback to the prior image-parameter pair. Cryptographic checksums verify the bit-level integrity of these stored calibration blocks, while physical self-tests at field release verify that the stored parameters still match the physical machine.

Finally, forensic traceability links every running OS image, policy checkpoint, and calibration partition to its training provenance, compiler flags, and reviewed HIL regression records through the signed release manifest (section 16.8). When an unexpected field intervention or trajectory excursion occurs, these links let incident investigators reconstruct the exact configuration running at that millisecond and determine which safety warrants need re-examination. While a cryptographic digest confirms identity, isolating the physical root cause requires detailed sensor traces and causal analysis.

16.9.2 Staging candidate policies before release

Software simulation models timing and electrical behavior only to the accuracy of its mathematical approximations; it cannot catch memory-bus contention, fieldbus noise, or inverter thermal sag on the real processor and drive electronics. A candidate policy must therefore repeat the hardware-in-the-loop (HIL) regression gates affected by its changes on a physical test bench under full thermal and electrical load. The bench tests the specific interference and fault mechanisms on which release claims depend:

  1. Worst-case execution time (WCET) and scheduling jitter: Profiling whether safety filter evaluations complete within their hard real-time deadlines (\(\WorstCasePermissionCycleTime \le \CompletionDeadline\)) while neural workloads drive peak memory crossbar contention and DMA transfers, the falsification campaign of Empirical Verification of Silicon Isolation.
  2. Deterministic bus arbitration: Measuring fieldbus communication jitter and cyclic transmission integrity across physical EtherCAT or CAN FD networks under simulated telemetry broadcast floods and electrical noise injection.
  3. Power-stage thermal dynamics and current ripple: Verifying that high-frequency control chatter or torque ripple proposed by the candidate policy does not induce excessive inverter switching losses, DC rail voltage sag, or thermal runaway in motor windings (Thermal Duty Cycles).
  4. Hardware fault-injection survivability: Validating that physical line breaks, power supply brownouts, or deserializer frame dropouts deterministically trigger the hardware watchdog and safe-stop sequence within certified timing budgets before motor power stages can be damaged.

A candidate policy advances through bench staging only when every required regression test passes and empirical records confirm that timing and physical margins remain intact. Observing zero deadline overruns during a finite bench campaign does not by itself establish a mathematical upper bound on worst-case execution time, nor does it show how the policy will behave on the warehouse floor. In cloud software engineering, blue-green deployment can reduce downtime by preparing a replacement cluster and redirecting traffic after readiness checks. In physical AI, however, blue-green cutover cannot happen mid-stride or at speed. Swapping active control authority between an incumbent controller and a candidate policy while mass is moving requires managing actuator state continuity, motor winding back-EMF, and dynamic tracking tubes (Command steps and mechanical drivetrain shock). De-energizing motor windings or inducing sudden torque steps causes severe mechanical shock loads (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia \LinkAngularAcceleration\)) and loss of traction. An unvalidated policy also cannot be granted partial motor authority in a populated warehouse to “canary test” edge-case performance.

Safe predeployment staging instead relies on shadow mode deployment, the shadow-operation evidence regime of The four evaluation evidence regimes applied to a release candidate. The incumbent controller \(\pi_{\text{incumbent}}\) supplies proposals, while the independent enforcer retains sole actuator-output ownership and checks each command before fieldbus dispatch. Concurrently in the background, the candidate neural policy \(\pi_{\text{candidate}}\) executes on the host compute SoC, consuming identical live sensor streams \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\) within its qualified shared-resource budget and computing what control command \(\CandidateControlCommand_t \sim \pi_{\text{candidate}}(\MultimodalObservation_{t})\) it would have proposed. The candidate writes its outputs to an isolated telemetry buffer that grants no actuator authority. Consumers can read that record, but neither the buffer nor the candidate can bypass the enforcer. Shadow inference and recording must stay within the interference and power budgets that preserve the active case. Discrepancies between candidate proposals and the incumbent’s admitted commands are logged as high-rate telemetry events without admitting any candidate command to the drives.

Shadow-mode telemetry monitors two instantaneous divergence metrics:

  1. Action discrepancy (\(\FiniteChange\ControlCommand_t\)): The Euclidean (or angular) distance between the candidate proposal and the incumbent control action, measured here by the Euclidean norm: \[\|\FiniteChange\ControlCommand_t\|_2 = \| \CandidateControlCommand_t - \IncumbentControlCommand_t \|_2.\] Sustained spikes in \(\|\FiniteChange\ControlCommand_t\|_2\) identify behavioral divergence where the candidate policy introduces unmodeled bias, high-frequency control jitter, or erratic steering commands. In addition, the discrepancy analyzer monitors the command-rate norm \(\|\CandidateControlCommandDerivative_t\|_2\), which is a torque-rate norm only when \(\ControlCommand\) is a torque command, to detect unmodeled control chatter that would excite mechanical resonances.

  2. Safety barrier margin discrepancy (\(\FiniteChange \BarrierFunctionRate_t\)): The differential proximity to forward-invariant safety boundaries under a control barrier function \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\) (Safe Sets as Conditional Permission): \[\FiniteChange \BarrierFunctionRate_t = \BarrierFunctionRate(\EstimatedPlantState_t, \CandidateControlCommand_t) - \BarrierFunctionRate(\EstimatedPlantState_t, \IncumbentControlCommand_t),\] where \(\EstimatedPlantState_t\) is the state estimated from the sensor streams \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\). A negative \(\FiniteChange \BarrierFunctionRate_t\) means the candidate has a smaller modeled barrier derivative than the incumbent. Both derivatives can still be positive, so the sign alone does not imply decreasing clearance or a violated boundary of \(\ForwardInvariantSafeSet\) (defined by \(\BarrierFunction(\MechanicalPlantState)\)).

The two discrepancies answer different questions. A large action discrepancy \(\|\FiniteChange\ControlCommand_t\|_2\) can reflect an alternate useful action, while \(\FiniteChange \BarrierFunctionRate_t\) indicates how the proposals differ in their modeled effect on the barrier. Neither sign alone supplies a verdict; review must consider the current margin, uncertainty, and the condition that the independent enforcer would require.

Shadow mode nevertheless has a limit that more traces cannot remove, the trajectory confinement of that evidence regime, which a release reviewer encounters as counterfactual divergence. Because admitted incumbent proposals determine the plant trajectory, the historical state trajectory \((\DecisionProcessState_0, \IncumbentControlCommand_0, \DecisionProcessState_1, \IncumbentControlCommand_1, \dots)\) is conditioned entirely on incumbent actions, and the candidate’s proposals \(\CandidateControlCommand_t\) never alter it. Conflating open-loop agreement with deployment safety is the open-loop replay fallacy: assuming that high action agreement during passive trace replay guarantees closed-loop dynamic stability. The \(\CovariateDriftBound\) result in Recovery and Compounding Error is an expected-cost bound under its stated imitation-learning assumptions, not a universal law for physical tracking error. A candidate that agrees on incumbent trajectories can still visit unfamiliar states or excite unmodeled dynamics when it closes the loop, the covariate shift that interactive imitation learning was designed to correct (Ross et al. 2011). Shadow mode is an essential pre-filter for gross behavioral anomalies, but never a substitute for closed-loop dynamic qualification.

Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.

12 Canary (the miners’ sentinel bird): Miners carried caged canaries because the birds succumb to carbon monoxide before people do, so a bird’s distress warned the crew to leave. The metaphor carries this section’s warning. The canary is the unit exposed to the hazard first, so on a physical fleet its exposure must be bounded by containment, not by how few units are chosen.

Phased canary fleet rollouts12 expose the candidate to closed-loop operation under a separately supported restricted case. Selecting fewer machines limits exposure but does not bound the consequence of any one action. The restrictions must address the physical hazard:

  1. Geofenced operational containment: Canary units operate exclusively within dedicated, surveyed low-risk corridors, such as wide transit aisles devoid of blind rack corners, reduced pedestrian density, and high-friction surveyed concrete (\(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge\) 0.60).
  2. Enforced kinematic derating: The real-time safety enforcer clamps candidate policy velocity and acceleration below nominal ratings (\(\SpeedAtDetection \le \CanarySpeedBound < \NominalRatedSpeed\)), expanding stopping distance margins and suppressing transient kinetic energy.
  3. Supervised human oversight: High-readiness human supervisors equipped with wireless remote emergency-stop (tele-stop) pendants maintain line-of-sight monitoring over canary units, backed by an active-lease watchdog.
  4. Targeted regression observability: The trial measures tracking error variance \(\CandidateTrackingErrorStandardDeviation^2\), drive torque ripple, and enforcer intervention frequency to reveal service regressions. The permission path retains only the containment claims validated for the restricted envelope.

Expansion requires predeclared acceptance criteria tied to the service and physical claims. Intervention-rate non-inferiority13 and tracking variance \(\CandidateTrackingErrorStandardDeviation^2 \le \IncumbentTrackingErrorStandardDeviation^2\) are possible capability criteria, with their comparison conditions and statistical assumptions stated. The non-inferiority margin \(\NoninferiorityRatioMargin\) is fixed before the trial, so that the upper one-sided 95 percent confidence bound on the rate ratio \(\CanaryInterventionRate/\IncumbentInterventionRate\) must fall below \(1+\NoninferiorityRatioMargin\). With a zero margin, that upper-bound criterion requires evidence that the candidate is better, which may demand more exposure than a small canary provides. The trial arithmetic of Trial Budgets, Stopping Rules, and Comparisons sets how much operation a given \(\NoninferiorityRatioMargin\) requires. A sequential test can stop expansion as soon as the evidence suffices. None of these criteria replaces the evidence for stopping, observation, or containment. An enforcer intervention also needs causal review, because suppressing legitimate clamps to improve a rollout metric would weaken the release argument.

13 Non-Inferiority Testing: The design comes from clinical trials, where failing to find a difference proves nothing, so a new treatment must instead be shown worse than the standard by no more than a margin fixed before any data arrive (Blackwelder 1982). A canary can therefore pass on modest exposure without proving the candidate better, but only if \(\NoninferiorityRatioMargin\) was not chosen after the result was seen.

Blackwelder, William C. 1982. “‘Proving the Null Hypothesis’ in Clinical Trials.” Controlled Clinical Trials 3 (4): 345–53. https://doi.org/10.1016/0197-2456(82)90024-1.

↩︎ The sequential probability ratio test, which stops a trial campaign as soon as the evidence crosses a precalibrated boundary, is established in Sequential probability ratio testing.

16.9.3 From qualification to a new manifest

Reducing a policy’s hesitation rate at the pick station does not justify relaxing its contact-force limits or floor-friction restrictions. If the retrained mug-delivery candidate were promoted on task speed alone, it would bypass the containment premises that keep the restricted route safe. Instead, the reviewer repeats closed-loop capability evaluation under the restricted route and feed conditions, and repeats the loaded timing and fault tests whose dependencies changed. Unaffected plant evidence remains reusable only while configuration, calibration, wear, and site premises still match.

Assume, still within the stipulated branch, that those results satisfy the predeclared capability criteria and preserve the containment claims. The adjudicator issues a new manifest binding the candidate, retained and repeated evidence, current calibration, and the same restricted mission. At the assessed stationary cutover state, the gate authenticates that manifest and checks current standing conditions before admitting new proposals. Field monitoring then compares the running service with the same success, cycle-time, rescue, and protection criteria. Failed qualification leaves the incumbent case unchanged; a failed standing condition withdraws the affected grant.

16.9.4 Over-the-air staging and watchdog-enforced rollbacks

A candidate written to the inactive bank inherits no operating grant from its predecessor, and rolling back to the prior software image cannot repair a worn brake or a stale calibration. Hardware watchdog rollback triggers must therefore distinguish a failure to boot from a failure during motion. Before tracking is enabled, failed image authentication, startup tests, or fieldbus synchronization can reject the candidate and select the retained image, which must still pass current calibration and standing-condition checks before receiving a permit.

During motion, the independent supervisor first commands the assessed fallback within its measured response budget. Where that response ends in torque removal, the supervisor asserts Safe Torque Off after the required controlled stop; a plant needing a powered hold retains the authority needed for that hold. Unlike a conventional compute watchdog that resets the processor immediately upon a timeout, a missed enforcer heartbeat withdraws tracking permission and triggers the hardware-controlled stop without resetting the processor mid-motion. Only after the plant has come to rest in its validated update state may the boot selector change banks.

The image and its compatible calibration schema form the rollback unit. If either record is missing, corrupted, or physically stale, the gate selects REFUSE unless a separately signed restricted case covers the actual state and its limits, such as \(\SpeedAtDetection \le \RestrictedSpeedBound\). Retaining the prior binary image provides an immediate rollback target, but operators must re-validate standing physical conditions before the recovered binary receives an operating permit.

16.9.5 Event-triggered telemetry and safety performance indicators

An enforcer trip can reveal a policy failure, a changed physical premise, or both, and the field record must let the reviewer distinguish those causes before choosing retraining, maintenance, or a narrower envelope. Because wireless summaries alone cannot reconstruct the observations and actuator response around the event, detailed traces are retained locally. Edge event-triggered telemetry captures the sensor, proposal, permission, actuator, and timestamp chain around an intervention or boundary excursion. A local rolling buffer preserves the lead-up, and capture continues through the physical response whenever the qualified recording power path survives. Buffer length, write bandwidth, and hold-up energy are sized from the required reconstruction window and verified under the same contention and power faults as the permission path. A logic-rail capacitor does not guarantee that the full sensor and storage path survives, and recording must never delay the stop.

UL 4600 calls the field measurements that keep a claim alive safety performance indicators (SPIs). Each is a metric tied to a named claim, with a threshold and a predeclared response when the threshold is crossed (UL Standards & Engagement 2023). On the running machine, every standing condition of section 16.5 has one. Measured stop distance at scheduled trials and enforcer trips per operating hour watch the base’s stopping claim, age refusals per operating hour watch the observation-age claim, and localization residuals against their allowance watch the sensor geometry. An SPI whose threshold is crossed withdraws or narrows the grant it watches; one that drifts toward its threshold triggers review before it is crossed, which is the defense against the normalization of deviance of section 16.7.

During routine operation, compact health summaries support fleet monitoring while required incident records retain full provenance locally until transfer succeeds. Learning-data filters may prioritize unfamiliar events for training, provided they never discard required safety evidence or hide repeated failures. Once the traces arrive, a reviewer distinguishes policy hesitation from calibration drift, surface contamination, and mechanism wear before assigning the repair: qualified recovery traces can enter the experience pipeline, whereas reconstructed simulations extend test coverage only over their modeled conditions. A captured event therefore changes the release argument only after its physical cause and operating conditions are established. Reconstructed scenes and retraining can improve a candidate policy, yet neither automatically repairs a physical warrant, so the revised claim must return through the relevant qualification stages before a new manifest grants authority. Because the traces the machine keeps also record the people around it, each record that identifies a person carries standing conditions of its own: a declared purpose, a retention period, and a list of readers, audited like any other premise.

↪ The declarations that govern records identifying people, and the access and retention audit that enforces them, are developed in Recorded people as a standing condition.

16.9.6 Fleet evidence and accountable release

Teams break the safety warrant when they pool dissimilar routes into a single fleet mileage total or treat an operator intervention count as a collision rate. Fleet exposure can reveal recurring failures, but interpreting it requires knowing the operating domain, the intervention definition, and the exact system configuration. The release reviewer uses fleet records to challenge the same capability and containment claims, rather than replacing them with a mileage ranking. One public fleet record shows what an interpretable version looks like. Waymo publishes its rider-only crash rates by outcome, each against a human-driven benchmark adjusted to the same locations and each with a confidence interval. It republishes them as rider-only mileage accumulates (Waymo 2026; Kusano et al. 2025). Figure 4 plots the latest release and the history of two of its outcomes.

Kusano, Kristofer D., John M. Scanlon, Yin-Hsiu Chen, Timothy L. McMurry, Tilia Gode, and Trent Victor. 2025. “Comparison of Waymo Rider-Only Crash Rates by Crash Type to Human Benchmarks at 56.7 Million Miles.” Traffic Injury Prevention 26 (sup1). https://doi.org/10.1080/15389588.2025.2499887.

In the June 2026 release, after 271 million rider-only miles, the injury estimate rests on 183 crashes and its interval spans a reduction of 79 to 85 percent. The serious-injury estimate rests on 3 crashes and spans 83 to 99 percent, and the fatal estimate rests on 2 and spans 18 to 98 percent. The same mileage therefore licenses a tight claim about common crashes and only a loose one about the outcomes a release decision cares about most, because rare events accumulate slowly however many miles the fleet drives. That is the fourth law, permission must be earned (principle 4), measured on a real fleet. Each outcome’s claim may extend only as far as its own interval, and a reviewer reads the interval before the point estimate.

The signed release manifest records the engineering decision and its accountable owner, and together with the records it binds, it lets an investigator follow one admitted action back to the person who signed for it. The enforcement record logs the admitted command. The manifest in force at that moment names by digest the policy weights, the enforcer and real-time operating system binaries, the calibration tables, and the operating envelope behind it. The signature on that manifest, made with a key held in a hardware security module, is bound to the sign-off record that names the lead systems adjudicator who accepted that configuration or the update that installed it. The manifest does not itself establish regulatory compliance or determine legal liability. Applicable requirements depend on the machine, intended use, and jurisdiction; the release manifest must preserve the evidence needed for that separate assessment. Throughout this lifecycle, release remains a decision about a named machine under named conditions. Shadow traces, staged trials, and field records can strengthen the next argument only when their operating conditions and limits are explicit, while the manifest and independent permission path retain the restrictions that the evidence supports.

↪ Who answers for each decision the manifest binds, and the duty to report an incident completely and on time, are developed in The Approval Record.

16.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

In purely digital software, a deployment bug manifests as a silent crash, a corrupted database row, or a dropped network packet; in physical AI, software decisions drive heavy actuators through real space where errors bend steel, destroy machinery, and injure coworkers. Releasing an embodied AI system therefore requires uncovering hidden assumptions before kinetic energy is coupled to physical mass. The following fallacies and pitfalls highlight recurring mistakes where teams confuse software statistical confidence with cyber-physical safety.
